* **The literal injection path**: a way to place a chosen instruction word
  without disturbing the neighbouring slot was not found.
* **The mixed fp20/fx10 writers and CSEL edge cases** stay where §13 left them.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-send` and `verify-alu-send` hold 109 rows each
(35 variants, three repeats, plus the four control runs), pinned at
`8996a2094b52...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical, and
`clean-alu-send` repeats the same 109 rows from a clone with a clean tree.

## 17. PSEQ fetch across draw boundaries

`asm_characterize.pseq_split.json` (12 variants), templates `asm/pseq_split_a.in`
(tag writer), `asm/pseq_split_b.in` (fetch and observe only) and
`asm/pseq_split_c.in` (second picture), plus the section 14 template as the
same-draw reference.

### The question

Section 14 tagged every pixel with its own window position, but the same
fragment stored the tag and fetched it back in the same draw. A fetch that
returned the store it had just made, or the register values it still held,
would have produced the same identity map. The question here is whether a fetch
performed by a *later* draw, which writes nothing to the source surface, reads
the surface at the fetching fragment's own pixel.

Four explanations to separate:

* **position** - the address is the fetching fragment's window position, so the
  map is the identity whatever happened before;
* **dw state** - the address is the one the DW last used, in draw A, so the map
  is one repeated word or a picture anchored at draw A's last pixel;
* **surface** - memory is addressed with the wrong pitch or tile, so the map is
  shifted or scrambled rather than identity;
* **nothing** - a fetch after a draw boundary returns nothing, so the
  observation surface shows only what draw B's registers held.

### Design

Draw A writes `tag(x, y) = 0xBF9F <y+1> <x+1>` to the source surface and
fetches nothing. Draw B fetches the same surface word and stores the result to
the linear rt4 surface, and stores nothing to the source. Both draws are the
same full-screen quad, so every pixel of a genuine 64x64 target is written by A
and every pixel of the observed rectangle is written by B. rt4's whole 64x64
rectangle is dumped by `GRATE_REGION` and its digest is the reported result.

Draw B preloads pattern B (`0x9F607F40`) into r0/r1 before the fetch, so a
fetch that does not happen shows B, an unwritten source shows zero, a fetched
tag shows the tag, and the changed-source control shows C (`0x10703020`).

Three test-tooling additions in `tools/assembler.c`, opt-in like section 15's:

* `GRATE_SIZE=64` allocates every surface (framebuffer, rt3, rt4, rt5) as a
  genuine 64x64 target instead of drawing a 64x64 window of a 256x256 one;
* `GRATE_PITCH_PAD=16` allocates the rt5 source 16 texels wider than the target;
* `GRATE_EXTRA_FS=a.in:b.in` draws the same quad again with each fragment
  program in turn, after the first draw is flushed, keeping the render targets
  and the vertex setup.

With `GRATE_SIZE=64` the vertex shader is `asm/vs_mov.txt`, the identity
transform, because `vs_scale_offset.txt` bakes a 0.25 scale that only fills a
quarter of the target at this size.

### Measured

| variant | digest | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `same-draw` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | section 14's reference picture at 64x64 |
| `two-draw` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | draw A writes, draw B fetches: the same picture |
| `two-draw-nofetch` | `0x8086DDC5` | pattern B everywhere: the fetch did not happen |
| `drawb-only` | `0x76EFDDC5` | `nz=0`, the unwritten source reads zero |
| `changed` | `0x54FFDDC5` | pattern C everywhere |
| `shift-a` | `0x80261DC5` | `tag(x+16, y)` at pixel `(x, y)` |
| `pair-23` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | the same map through the r2/r3 destination pair |
| `src-rt1` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | identity through the tiled framebuffer |
| `src-rt3-linear` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | identity through linear rt3 |
| `src-rt3-tiled` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | identity through tiled rt3 |
| `src-rt5-rgb565` | `0xAB4CFDC5` | 16-bit source: the map completes, one coordinate byte is lost |
| `pitch-pad-16` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | identity through a source 16 texels wider than the target |

Every pixel of the identity variants was also checked against the formula
`0xBF9F0000 | ((y+1) << 8) | (x+1)`, not only against a digest:

| variant | pixels checked | mismatches |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `two-draw` | 4096 | 0 |
| `shift-a` (formula `x+17` in the low byte) | 4096 | 0 |
| `pitch-pad-16` | 4096 | 0 |
| `src-rt1` | 4096 | 0 |
| `src-rt3-tiled` | 4096 | 0 |

`changed`'s digest is the same `0x54FFDDC5` that section 14's `last-store`
variant produced for a surface holding C everywhere, which ties the two sets'
picture digests together. `src-rt5-rgb565`'s first row is `FF9C0000` for every
column, row 8 starts `FF9C0800` and row 63 `FF9C4100`: the byte carrying `y+1`
survives quantised to 5 bits (`64 -> 0x41`, `8 -> 0x08`), the byte carrying
`x+1` is dropped, and the columns are identical. That is the format, not the
addressing, and it agrees with section 15's rgb565 channel finding.

### What this settles

On this device and setup:

* a PSEQ fetch performed by a later draw reads the source surface where draw A
  left it, at the fetching fragment's own window position, with the program's
  destination pair;
* the map does not depend on the address the preceding DW used: `shift-a` shows
  the picture moved by exactly the 16 columns draw A was shifted by, while a
  carried-over address would have produced a constant word or a picture
  anchored at draw A's last pixel;
* an unwritten source reads zero, and a fetch that does not execute leaves the
  preloaded pattern B, so the three pictures - tag, C, B, zero - are distinct;
* the same identity map comes back through a tiled framebuffer, linear and
  tiled rt3, and an rt5 surface allocated 16 texels wider than the target, so
  the fetch uses the target's tile layout and pitch rather than assuming the
  target width times bytes per pixel.

### What this does not settle

* **The mechanism.** As in section 14, the address may come from the position
  registers or from the fragment's own target coordinate; draw B stores only to
  rt4, which separates it from the *source* surface's DW address, but rt4's
  address is still the same pixel. A fetch from a fragment covering a different
  pixel of the source than the one it stores to is untested.
* **Two draws without a flush between them.** The harness flushes after each
  draw. Section 23 issues both draws in one submission and finds the same map,
  so the explicit flush is not what makes the earlier draw visible on this
  setup.
* **A later draw that also writes the source.** Draw B writes nothing to it, so
  the read-after-write hazard of section 14 is not combined with the draw
  boundary here.
* **Padded tiled pitch.** `GRATE_PITCH_PAD` was only applied to the linear rt5
  source here. Section 23 pads a tiled rt3 and gets the identity map and the
  same shifted picture, so the tile layout, not the allocation width, sets the
  row stride.
* **Targets larger than 64x64 and coordinates past 63.** The genuine target is
  64x64; section 14 covered a 64x64 window at (32,16) of a 256x256 target at
  the same time the set was run, but not with this tag range.
* **The 16-bit source.** Only one coordinate byte survives in rgb565, so that
  variant is a completion and format control, not a readable address map.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-pseq-split` and `verify-pseq-split` hold 40 rows
each (12 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`bb4b6b872647...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.
`clean-pseq-split` repeats the same 40 rows from a clone of the commit.

## 18. Which ALU marker does what

`asm_characterize.alu_marks.json` (76 variants), template `asm/alu_marks.in`
(four packets: primes, the instructions under test, and two store packets).
Packet 0 primes r2 with 0.25 (`0x40`) and r3 with 0.75 (`0xBF`), so an unwritten
destination is still readable.

Two instructions, identical in every variant:

    MAD  r2.l*,  u2,  #1,  #0     sends 0.25, writes r2
    MAD  r3.l*,  u0,  #1,  #0     receives 0.0625 + accumulator, writes r3

`store rt1, r2, r3` reads both destinations in one word,
`[r3.hi][r3.lo][r2.hi][r2.lo]`: `0x00100040` is "nothing happened" (r2 = 0.25,
r3 = 0.0625), `0x00500040` is "the receiver got 0.25" (r3 = 0.3125), and the
sender's own destination is `0x40` unless the marker changed it.

### The matrix

All twelve ordered slot pairs, each with all four marker combinations
(`nn` neither, `sn` sender only, `nr` receiver only, `sr` both). The transfer
sets:

| combination | pairs that transfer |
|:--|:--|
| `nn` | none |
| `sn` | (1,0) (2,0) (3,2) |
| `nr` | (0,1) (1,2) (2,3) (3,0) |
| `sr` | (0,1) (1,0) (1,2) (2,0) (2,3) (3,0) (3,2) |

`sr` is exactly the set section 16 measured with both markers, and it is the
union of `sn` and `nr`: the two markers do not need each other and each has its
own routing. The sender's own destination stayed `0x40` in all 48 cells, so in
a single-pair packet the sender's result is unaffected by either marker.

### What each marker does

**`(this)` on slot j** adds the *previous slot's own result* to slot j's MAD
result, wrapping at the packet start (slot 0 takes slot 3). It works with the
sender unmarked: `nr-s0r1` transfers although the sender carries no marker. The
set is exactly `j <- j-1 (mod 4)`.

**`(other)` on slot i** delivers slot i's own result into the accumulator of
slot `(i-1) & ~1`: slot 0 from slots 1 and 2, slot 2 from slot 3, and nowhere
from slot 0. That target instruction adds the value **whether or not it is
marked `(this)`**: `sn-s2r0` transfers with an unmarked receiver. The rule fits
all twelve `sn` cells; the "low slot of the previous pair" reading is an
inference from those cells, not an established mechanism.

Both markers on one instruction therefore do two different things: receive from
the previous slot and send to the previous pair's low slot.

### Two senders into one receiver

Senders A (`0.25` into r0) and B (`0.5` into r1) both marked `(other)`, receiver
in r3 marked `(this)`; the `-acc` variant stores r2/r3 and the `-dest` variant
stores r0/r1:

| arrangement (A, B, receiver) | `-acc` | `-dest` | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 0, 1, 2 | `0x008F0040` | `0x007F00BF` | receiver got B (0.5) only; r0 = 0.75 |
| 0, 1, 3 | `0x00100040` | `0x007F00BF` | receiver got nothing; r0 = 0.75 |
| 0, 2, 3 | `0x008F0040` | `0x007F00BF` | receiver got B (0.5); r0 = 0.75 |
| 1, 2, 3 | `0x008F0040` | `0x007F0040` | receiver got B (0.5); r0 = 0.25 |

The receiver never receives both values: with two senders the only value it
takes is its previous slot's (`nr` rule), so `(0,1,2)` and `(0,2,3)` deliver B
and `(0,1,3)` delivers the NOP in slot 2. `(other)` at slot 1 or slot 2 puts
B's value into slot 0, whose *sender* instruction then writes `0.25 + 0.5 =
0.75` into r0, which is why the `-dest` word is `0xBF` and not `0x40`. When the
sender sits in slot 1 and the second sender in slot 2 (`1,2,3`), the send lands
on slot 0, which holds a NOP, and r0 keeps its plain `0.25`.

### Chaining

Sender S (`0.25` into r2), middle M (`0.5` into r1, both markers) and receiver R
(`0.0625` into r3, marked `(this)`):

| arrangement (S, M, R) | `-acc` | `-mid` | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 0, 1, 2 | `0x008F00BF` | `0x00BF0000` | r2 = 0.75, r1 = 0.75, r3 = 0.5625 |
| 0, 1, 3 | `0x001000BF` | `0x00BF0000` | r2 = 0.75, r1 = 0.75, r3 = 0.0625 |
| 0, 2, 3 | `0x008F00BF` | `0x007F0000` | r2 = 0.75, r1 = 0.5, r3 = 0.5625 |
| 1, 2, 3 | `0x008F0040` | `0x00BF0000` | r2 = 0.25, r1 = 0.75, r3 = 0.5625 |

Two separate facts come out of this:

* the middle slot does receive its previous slot's result when it sits directly
  after the sender (`0,1,x` and `1,2,3`: r1 = 0.75), and does not when a NOP
  sits between them (`0,2,3`: r1 = 0.5), which agrees with the `(this)` rule;
* what the middle slot *forwards* is its own bare MAD result, not the value it
  accumulated: in `0,1,2` the receiver gets `0.0625 + 0.5`, while r1 itself
  holds `0.5 + 0.25`. A sender's accumulated destination and the value it
  sends are therefore different numbers.

`chain-plain-acc` (`0x00100040`) and `chain-plain-mid` (`0x007F0000`) run the
same three instructions without markers: nothing is received and the middle
writes its bare result.

### The destination field and r0/r1

A single `MAD rN.l*, u2, #1, #0` (0.25) in each slot, stored as `rt1, r0, r1`:

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `dst-none` | `0x00000000` | no write: r0 and r1 stay zero |
| `dst-none-23` | `0x00BF0040` | the primes only |
| `dst-r0-s0` .. `s3` | `0x00000040` | r0 = 0.25 from every slot |
| `dst-r1-s0` .. `s3` | `0x00400000` | r1 = 0.25 from every slot |

With the matrix writing r2 and r3 from all twelve slot pairs, all four
registers `r0..r3` are written by hardware from any of the four slots. This is a
write-back result, not an assembler word: the values land in the registers the
mnemonic names.

### What this settles

* `(this)` and `(other)` are independent: the receiver's marker pulls the
  previous slot's result, the sender's marker delivers to slot 0 or 2, and the
  two sets union to section 16's matrix;
* an unmarked instruction accumulates a value sent to its slot, so "the
  receiver's accumulator" is a property of the slot, not only of the marker;
* a slot's accumulated destination and the value it forwards differ;
* r0 and r1 are written by hardware from every slot, completing the r0..r3
  range alongside the matrix's r2/r3.

### What this does not settle

* **Why `(other)` targets `(i-1) & ~1`.** The rule fits every cell, but the
  slot pairing behind it is inferred from the transfer set, not decoded from
  the control words.
* **`(other)` from slot 0.** Nothing in the packet receives it; whether it
  targets a previous packet's slot, the other ALU lane, or nothing at all is
  untested.
* **Three senders, or two receivers.** Only two senders and one receiver, and
  one middle slot, were run.
* **Cross-packet delivery.** Every arrangement here is inside one packet.
* **The value format.** The summed destination shows the fx10 add and the
  section 14 byte conversion (`0x40 + 0x80 = 0xC0 -> 0xBF` stored); the
  accumulator's precision for other operands is not swept.
* **Double accumulation.** `sr` transfers once, not twice, but the case of a
  slot that both receives from its previous slot and is sent to by `(other)` at
  the same time was not built.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-marks` and `verify-alu-marks` hold 232 rows
each (76 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`f38df6a66d8e...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.
`clean-alu-marks` repeats the same 232 rows from a clone of the commit.

## 19. TEX qualifications: mip ties, wrap state and varyings

`asm_characterize.tex_qual.json` (55 variants), templates `asm/tex_probe.in`
(constant coordinates) and `asm/tex_vary.in` (varying coordinates), with
`asm/vs_texcoord_ramp.txt` and `asm/linker_texcoord.txt` for the varying group.
Three test-tooling additions in `tools/assembler.c`:

* `GRATE_TEX_PIXEL=x,y,0xRRGGBBAA` paints one texel on a zeroed level 0 before
  the mip chain is generated, so every level has a different value at that
  texel;
* `GRATE_TEX_WRAP=clamp|repeat|mirror` sets the s and t wrap state explicitly;
* `GRATE_TEX_CHECKER` fills level 0 with alternating columns of black and white,
  whose 2x2 average is `0x7F`, so any level above 0 is flat.

Sampler state is part of each variant's environment and never part of the TEX
word: the word selects the sampler index, the destination pair and the
LOD/bias register (sections 11, 12 and 15), while the surface size, format,
min/mag filters, wrap state and mip chain come from the texture's own state.

### Nearest mip level at a tie

16x16 surface, one white texel at (0,0), `--mipmap`, min filter
`nearest-mipmap-nearest`, mag filter nearest, wrap repeat, s = t = 0.03125
(texel (0,0) centre). The level words first, so the fractions below name real
levels:

| LOD | word | level |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.0 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | 0 |
| 1.0 | `0xFF404040` | 1 |
| 2.0 | `0xFF121212` | 2 |
| 3.0 | `0xFF050505` | 3 |
| 4.0 | `0xFF050505` | 3 or 4 |

Level 4 reads the same word as level 3: a 16x16 chain's last level is 1x1, whose
value should be `0x01`, so either the chain stops at 2x2 or the sampler clamps
there. That is not separated here.

The fractions:

| LOD | word | level | LOD | word | level |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.25 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | 0 | 1.25 | `0xFF404040` | 1 |
| 0.4375 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | 0 | 1.4375 | `0xFF404040` | 1 |
| 0.5 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | 0 | 1.5 | `0xFF404040` | 1 |
| 0.5625 | `0xFF404040` | 1 | 1.5625 | `0xFF121212` | 2 |
| 0.75 | `0xFF404040` | 1 | 1.75 | `0xFF121212` | 2 |
| 2.4375 | `0xFF121212` | 2 | 2.5 | `0xFF121212` | 2 |
| 2.5625 | `0xFF050505` | 3 | | | |

Every tie goes to the **lower** level: 0.5 -> 0, 1.5 -> 1, 2.5 -> 2, and each
threshold sits between the tie and the next quarter step. The rule that fits all
thirteen fractions is `level = ceil(LOD - 0.5)`, i.e. round half down. Section
15's "ties-to-even fit" is withdrawn: it was fitted to a chain whose levels 1
and 2 held the same word, so 1.5 could not be seen to choose level 1.

The blending mip filter has no tie to round and interpolates linearly between
the two neighbouring levels by the fraction:

| LOD | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.0 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | level 0 |
| 0.5 | `0xFFA0A0A0` | halfway between `0xFF` and `0x40` |
| 1.0 | `0xFF404040` | level 1 |
| 1.5 | `0xFF292929` | halfway between `0x40` and `0x12` |
| 2.5 | `0xFF0C0C0C` | halfway between `0x12` and `0x05` |

### Coordinates outside [0,1] with an explicit wrap state

16x16 two-tone surface (left half black, right half white), mag filter nearest
unless stated, t = 0.5, s as tabulated. Point sampling picks one texel; the
index it computes is `u = s * 16 - 0.5` rounded to a texel, and what happens
outside the surface is the wrap state's decision:

| s | repeat (default) | clamp | mirror |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| -0.5 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` |
| 1.0 | `0x00000000` | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0xFFFFFFFF` |
| 1.25 | `0x00000000` | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0xFFFFFFFF` |
| 2.0 | `0x00000000` | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0x00000000` |

The three columns are three different answers for the same coordinate, so the
sampler honours an explicitly set wrap state and the default state is repeat
(section 11's reading, now measured against two other states rather than
inferred from one). Mirroring agrees with `31 - (n mod 32)` on these four
points: -8 -> 7 (black), 16 -> 15 (white), 20 -> 11 (white), 32 -> 0 (black).

The same question on t, on a row-colour surface (rows `0x000000FF`,
`0x0000FF00`, `0x00FF0000`, `0xFFFFFFFF`), at t = 1.125 and s = 0.875:

| wrap | word | row read |
|:--|:--|:--|
| repeat | `0x00FF0000` | row 18 -> row 2 |
| clamp | `0xFFFFFFFF` | row 15 |
| mirror | `0x0000FF00` | row 13 |

The linear filter's footprint uses the same wrap state, which is what separates
the two effects at the edge:

| s | repeat | clamp |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.0 | `0x80808080` | `0x00000000` |
| 0.0625 | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` |
| 0.9375 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0xFFFFFFFF` |
| 1.0 | `0x80808080` | `0xFFFFFFFF` |
| 1.25 | `0x00000000` | `0xFFFFFFFF` |

At s = 1.0 the two taps are texels 15 and 16. Under repeat texel 16 is texel 0,
so the word is a 50/50 blend (`0x80`); under clamp both taps are texel 15, so
the word is white. The filter always blends two taps - that is its footprint -
and the wrap state decides which texels those are.

### The varying reaches the fetch

Section 15 recorded that a varying coordinate "does not reach the fetch", from
a fetch that returned `0x00000000` and from nine s values returning the same
texel. That conclusion does not hold: the surface's left half is black, so
`0x00000000` is a valid sample, and only `fb_data[0]` was read. This set stores
to rt4 and digests the whole 64x64 region instead.

`asm/vs_texcoord_ramp.txt` exports the quad's `texcoord` attribute scaled by a
"scale" uniform, and `asm/linker_texcoord.txt` links it without the yxzw
swizzle, so r0 is the varying's x and r1 its y. The fetch reads r0/r1, and the
packet after the fetch stores them, which records the coordinates the fetch
used. At scale 4, row 32 of that dump starts

    FF00BF00 FF00CB00 FF00D100 FF00D500 FF00D800 FF00DA00 ...

i.e. r1 = `0xFF00` (fp20, about 1.47, constant in a row and so wrapping in t)
while the `s` byte rises 0xBF, 0xCB, 0xD1, ... to 0xF6 at the right edge: s
spans about 0 to 1.35 across the row. `vary-nofetch-s4` stores the same
registers with the fetch replaced by NOP and produces the same digest
(`0x45E4E1C5`), so the map difference below cannot come from the store alone.

Fetch maps on a 64x64 checker, `--mipmap`, min filter
`nearest-mipmap-nearest`, mag filter nearest, wrap repeat, whole rt4 digested:

| variant | scale | digest | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `vary-level0-s4` | 4 | `0xF11D7DC5` | no chain: the checker pattern |
| `vary-mip-s4` | 4 | `0xF11D7DC5` | chain present, still the pattern |
| `vary-mip-s1` | 1 | `0x238515C5` | a quarter the gradient, a wider pattern |
| `vary-mip-s8` | 8 | `0xDA85F6DD` | level 1: flat `0x80` over most rows |
| `vary-const-mip` | - | `0x20B6CDC5` | constant coordinates, one texel everywhere |

At scale 4 the checker's 32/32 column pattern is unchanged by the mip chain
(one texel per pixel, LOD 0), and at scale 8 most rows read `0xFF808080`, the
level-1 word, with two-column blends at the band edges. So the level the fetch
uses changes with the coordinate gradient: the sampler does estimate an LOD
from the derivative of a varying. Rows 0 and 32 of `vary-mip-s8` are the
exception: all 64 columns read `0x00000000`, which is neither the pattern nor
the flat level-1 word. That is unexplained and is left as a measurement, not
interpreted.

A consistency check, not a derivation: at scale 4 the measured ramp gives about
1.35 texels per pixel, and log2(1.35) = 0.43, below the 0.5 threshold, so level
0; at scale 8 it is about 2.7 texels per pixel and log2(2.7) = 1.43, so level
1. The two levels seen agree with an LOD of log2(du/dx), but the formula is not
established by two points and no attempt is made here to fit the derivative
estimate itself.

### What this settles

* a nearest mip filter rounds every tie **down**: 0.5, 1.5 and 2.5 choose levels
  0, 1 and 2 on a chain whose levels all differ, and `ceil(LOD - 0.5)` fits all
  thirteen fractions measured;
* the blending mip filter interpolates the neighbouring levels by the fraction,
  with no tie;
* the sampler honours an explicitly set wrap state, and the default is repeat;
* point sampling wraps, clamps or mirrors the texel index according to that
  state, and the linear filter's two taps follow the same state, so an
  out-of-range coordinate is attributed to the wrap state rather than to the
  filter's footprint;
* an interpolated varying does reach a TEX fetch in a later exec, and the level
  the fetch uses changes with the coordinate gradient.

### What this does not settle

* **The derivative formula.** Only two gradients produced different levels, and
  the two all-zero rows at scale 8 are unexplained. Section 20 measures the texel
  rate behind the ramp, locates the level-0 boundary, shows that the texture's
  dimensions do not enter the level, and identifies those rows as missing
  samples whose position follows the vertical ramp.
* **The level-4 word.** Level 3 and 4 read the same `0x05`; whether the chain
  stops at 2x2 or the sampler clamps is not separated.
* **Ties between non-quarter fractions.** The ladder is quarter steps; a tie at
  a fraction that is not exactly representable is untested.
* **Wrap state per axis.** `GRATE_TEX_WRAP` sets s and t together, so s-repeated
  with t-clamped is not measured.
* **Minification with a varying.** The checker test is at LOD 0 to 1; the
  minification range where the derivative dominates was not swept.
* **The coordinate mapping.** The varying's absolute scale (s spanning 1.35 at
  scale 4 rather than 4) follows the attribute and perspective path, which this
  set does not model; only the gradient, which is what the LOD needs, is used.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-tex-qual` and `verify-tex-qual` hold 169 rows each
(55 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`61bfc59c30e6...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.
`clean-tex-qual` repeats the same 169 rows from a clone of the commit.

## 20. The derivative that sets the mip level

`asm_characterize.tex_deriv.json` (40 variants), template `asm/tex_deriv.in`
(varying coordinates, five batches: the varying setup, an explicit level into
`r3`, the fetch, the store), the section 19 templates for the explicit-LOD
references, and `asm/vs_texcoord_ramp.txt` for the coordinate ramp.
`GRATE_TEX_STEPS` and `GRATE_TEX_GRID` are new opt-in surfaces in
`tools/assembler.c`.

### The question

Section 19 showed that the level a fetch uses changes with the coordinate
gradient, on two gradients, on a chain where only level 0 had its own word.
Four things were left open: which axes enter the estimate, how the level follows
the magnitude, whether the texture's dimensions matter, and how the two axes
combine. Five explanations to separate:

* **x only** - the level follows the horizontal rate and the vertical one is
  ignored;
* **max** - the level follows the larger of the two per-axis rates;
* **norm** - it follows the vector magnitude, `sqrt(du^2 + dv^2)`, which is half
  a level above max when the two axes have equal rates;
* **sum** - it follows `|du| + |dv|`, a whole level above max;
* **normalized** - the rate is measured in texture coordinates rather than
  texels, so the texture's dimensions do not enter.

### The readout, and what it can and cannot name

`GRATE_TEX_STEPS` builds a one-texel checker split into four vertical bands of
decreasing intensity (0xFF, 0xBF, 0x7F, 0x3F over black), so a fetch map should
show the level as a band structure: a checker at level 0, four flat bands at
level 1, two at level 2.

The explicit-LOD references show the chain does not do that. At a texel in the
first band and one in the third, through the section 19 path:

| explicit level | band 0 | band 2 |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0 | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0xFF7F7F7F` |
| 1 | `0xFF808080` | `0xFF404040` |
| 2 | `0xFF808080` | `0xFF3E3E3E` |
| 3 | `0xFF7E7E7E` | `0xFF383838` |

Levels 1, 2 and 3 hold the same band grey to within a rounding drift, because a
one-texel checker's 2x2 average is the same at every level: the surface
distinguishes **level 0 from level 1 and above**, and cannot name the level past
that. Every "level" below is therefore "left level 0" or "still level 0"; no
statement is made about which of levels 1..3 was chosen. This is a limitation of
the surface, not of the sampler, and it is why the ladder in `GRATE_TEX_COLUMNS`
was not used for this set either: a pattern whose block average is constant per
region is level-invariant.

### The texel rate, measured

`GRATE_TEX_GRID` fills a 4x4 block grid of 16-texel blocks on a 64x64 surface.
Fetching it with no chain at five scales and counting block boundaries along a
row and a column:

| scale | x boundaries | y boundaries |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 1 | 0 | 0 |
| 2 | 1 | 1 |
| 4 | 3 | 3 |
| 8 | 7 | 7 |
| 16 | 15 | 15 |

So the ramp spans `(2^k - 1) * 16` texels at scale `2^k`, i.e. **du/dx =
dv/dy = 0.25 * scale texels per pixel** on a 64x64 target with a 64x64 texture,
and the two axes behave identically here. The factor is a property of the
harness's varying setup, not of the shader's `scale` uniform: the shader
multiplies by 4 at scale 4 and the fetch sees a quarter of that.

### What the level does

With the x axis alone, seven magnitudes (`sweep-`), rate in texels per pixel in
brackets:

| rate | 0.5 | 0.71 | 1.0 | 1.41 | 2.0 | 2.83 | 4.0 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| map | checker | checker | checker | checker | bands | bands | bands |
| level | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 1+ | 1+ | 1+ |

The level leaves 0 between 1.41 and 2.0 texels per pixel. `ceil(LOD - 0.5)` with
`LOD = log2(rate)` puts that boundary exactly at 1.41, which ties down to level
0, and at 2.0 gives level 1 - so the x axis is consistent with the level being
`floor`-like in `log2(du/dx)`, with the tie rounding section 19 measured.

Both axes at the same rate as the last two single-axis cells:

| scale | rate per axis | map | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 4.595 | 1.149 | checker, identical to x-only | level 0 |
| 6.498 | 1.625 | four bands | level 1+ |

Equal rates of 1.149 in both axes stay at level 0, where `norm` would give
`log2(1.63) = 0.7` (level 1) and `sum` would give `log2(2.3) = 1.2` (level 1).
So on this pair the second axis does **not** add to the first: `max` fits and
`norm` and `sum` are excluded, subject to the vertical anomaly below.

### The vertical axis does not behave like the horizontal one

With the y axis alone the map does not show a band structure at all:

* at rate 1.625 (`axis-y-6p498`) the whole map is one word, `0xFF808080`, with a
  few `0xFF767676` at the right edge - no bands, no checker;
* at rate 1.149 (`axis-y-4p595`) it is a checker, i.e. level 0, but with a
  different phase from the x-only cell of the same rate.

So the level question can be answered for x and not for y with this harness. The
y measurements are recorded as an unexplained asymmetry: the same nominal rate
produces a level change on one axis and a filtered-looking map on the other. The
block-grid calibration says the *coordinate* ramps identically on both axes, so
the asymmetry is in what the sampler does with the vertical derivative, not in
the varying itself.

### Texture dimensions

The same scale on three square sizes, and on the non-square pair with one axis
ramping at a time:

| variant | size | scale | digest |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `size-128-128-s16` | 128x128 | 16, 16 | `0x9140D8C5` |
| `size-64-64-s16` | 64x64 | 16, 16 | `0x9140D8C5` |
| `size-32-32-s16` | 32x32 | 16, 16 | `0x9140D8C5` |
| `size-128x128-s4` | 128x128 | 4, 4 | `0x412EFDC5` |
| `size-32x32-s4` | 32x32 | 4, 4 | `0x412EFDC5` |
| `oblong-64x32-x16` | 64x32 | 16, 1 | `0x0587C1C5` |
| `oblong-32x64-x16` | 32x64 | 16, 1 | `0x0587C1C5` |
| `sweep-x-16` | 64x64 | 16, 1 | `0x0587C1C5` |
| `oblong-64x32-y16` | 64x32 | 1, 16 | `0xF84DB9C5` |
| `oblong-32x64-y16` | 32x64 | 1, 16 | `0xF84DB9C5` |

At scale 16 all three square sizes give the *same digest*. That is what a
texel-based estimate predicts: if the level rises by one per doubling of the
texture and the sampled grid is `size / 2^level`, the product is invariant, so
the map is identical. A normalized estimate would keep the level and widen the
footprint in texture coordinates, and the maps would differ. The 32-wide and
64-wide non-square surfaces give the x-gradient digest of the 64x64 surface
exactly, so the width does not enter the x estimate either, and the same holds
for the y gradients on 64x32 and 32x64.

### The zero rows

Section 19 left two rows that read `0x00000000` unexplained. They are
reproducible (`zero-checker-s8` is `0xDA85F6DD`, bit-identical to section 19's
`vary-mip-s8`), and which rows they are depends on the vertical ramp:

| variant | vertical ramp | all-zero rows |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `sweep-x-8`, `zero-steps-s8-y1` | none | 0..7 |
| `zero-checker-s8`, `zero-steps-s8` | 8 | 0, 32 |
| `axis-y-6p498` | 6.498 | 20, 59 |

On the steps surface no level above 0 holds a black texel, and the zero rows
appear there too, so they are **missing samples, not a mip level**: on those
rows the fetch returned nothing at all. Rows 0..7 with a flat vertical ramp and
rows 0 and 32 with a steep one place them with the coordinate path rather than
with the level.

### What this settles

On this device and setup:

* the varying ramp gives du/dx = dv/dy = 0.25 * scale texels per pixel, measured
  by counting block boundaries, symmetric in both axes;
* with a horizontal gradient the level leaves 0 between 1.41 and 2.0 texels per
  pixel, which is where `log2(du/dx)` with section 19's round-half-down lands;
* equal gradients in both axes stay at the level the *larger* axis alone
  produces, so the second axis does not add in quadrature or linearly;
* the level does not change with the texture's dimensions: the same scale gives
  the same map on 32, 64 and 128 square surfaces, and the same x-gradient map on
  32, 64 and 64-wide non-square surfaces, which is what a texel-based estimate
  with a compensated sampling grid predicts;
* the section 19 zero rows are reproducible missing samples whose rows follow
  the vertical ramp, not a mip level.

### What this does not settle

* **The level above 1.** The steps surface is level-invariant above level 1, so
  no statement is made about levels 2, 3 or 4 in any variant, and the exact
  LOD expression beyond "level 0 or above" is not measured.
* **The vertical axis.** A vertical-only gradient produces a single blended word
  instead of a band structure at the same rate. Whether the vertical derivative
  enters the estimate, and how, is unresolved; the `max` reading above rests on
  the horizontal cells.
* **The two rows of the quad.** A screen-aligned quad has `du/dy = dv/dx = 0`,
  so no measurement here separates "max over the two axes" from "max over the
  two rows of the derivative pair".
* **Minification beyond level 1.** The sweep stops at 4 texels per pixel; a
  larger gradient on both axes (`sweep-both-16`) changes the map again but the
  surface cannot say to which level.
* **The absolute constant.** The 0.25 in the rate is the harness's varying path
  (section 19's caveat), not the shader's factor, and the expression is only
  established up to that constant.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-tex-deriv` and `verify-tex-deriv` hold 124 rows
each (40 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`50c89582e601...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.

## 21. Mip ties across levels, and the two wrap axes separately

`asm_characterize.tex_state.json` (59 variants), template `asm/tex_probe.in`
(constant coordinates, the fetch word, an explicit level in `r3`), the grid and
step surfaces in `tools/assembler.c`.

### The questions

Section 19 measured the nearest-mip tie at 0.5, 1.5 and 2.5 on a 16x16 chain in
quarter steps, and set the wrap state on both axes at once. Three things were
left: whether the rule holds on a chain whose levels all differ, how close to the
tie a value can sit before the level changes, and whether the two axes' wrap
states are independent. Two explanations to separate for the last one:

* **one state** - the sampler keeps a single wrap state and setting one axis
  changes both, so a mixed test reads one axis twice;
* **per axis** - each axis is wrapped by its own state, so a mixed test names
  each axis separately.

TEX instruction-word semantics are untouched: every variant uses the same fetch
word, `0x00002420`, and only the sampler state and the level register vary.

### The level words

32x32 surface with one white texel (`GRATE_TEX_PIXEL=0,0,0xFFFFFFFF`), point
sampling at texel (0,0), nearest mip filter, explicit level:

| level | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| word | `0xFFFFFFFF` | `0xFF404040` | `0xFF121212` | `0xFF050505` | `0xFF020202` |

Five distinct words, so every tie below names a level unambiguously. On the
16x16 chain of section 19 levels 3 and 4 held the same `0x05`, which is why the
tie at 3.5 could not be read there; here level 4 is `0x02`, so a 3.5 tie would
be readable. It was not run.

### The ties

| LOD | level | word |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.4375 | 0 | `0xFFFFFFFF` |
| 0.5 | 0 | `0xFFFFFFFF` |
| 0.5625 | 1 | `0xFF404040` |
| 1.4375 | 1 | `0xFF404040` |
| 1.5 | 1 | `0xFF404040` |
| 1.5625 | 2 | `0xFF121212` |
| 2.4375 | 2 | `0xFF121212` |
| 2.5 | 2 | `0xFF121212` |
| 2.5625 | 3 | `0xFF050505` |

The tie goes to the lower level at all three boundaries, which is section 19's
result on a chain where the levels are all different rather than on one where
levels 1 to 4 looked alike. The 64x64 chain reproduces the 1.5 case exactly
(`tie64-near-1p5` = `0x4E67DDC5` = `tie32-near-1p5`), so the rule does not
depend on the chain's length or on the level's texel count.

### How close to the tie the level changes

Section 19's quarter steps could not say whether the boundary is exactly at the
half-integer. Binary fractions around each tie:

| LOD | level | LOD | level |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.5 | 0 | 0.5078125 | 0 |
| 0.515625 | 1 | 0.53125 | 1 |
| 1.4921875 | 1 | 1.49609375 | 1 |
| 1.5 | 1 | 1.501953125 | 1 |
| 1.50390625 | 1 | 1.5078125 | 1 |
| 1.515625 | 2 | 1.53125 | 2 |
| 2.5 | 2 | 2.5078125 | 2 |
| 2.515625 | 3 | 2.53125 | 3 |

So the level changes **1/64 above the half-integer**, at every tie, and not at
the tie itself. Two descriptions fit every cell: round to nearest with the
boundary at `k + 0.515625` (that is `floor(LOD + 31/64)`), or round to nearest
after quantising the level to 1/32 with a half-up carry. The set does not
separate those - a value at 1.515625 is the same either way - so the measured
statement is the bracket, not the mechanism.

### The two wrap axes

`GRATE_TEX_GRID` gives a 16x16 surface of 4x4 blocks whose words are
`0xFF<r><g>00`, the red byte naming the block column and the green byte the block
row (block 0 is `0x30`, block 3 `0xC0`). Point sampling at s = t = 1.25 puts the
texel index at 20 in both axes, which repeat reads as block 1, clamp as block 3
and mirrored repeat as `31 - 20 = 11`, block 2. All nine combinations:

| wrap s | wrap t | word | block (s, t) |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| repeat | repeat | `0xFF606000` | 1, 1 |
| repeat | clamp | `0xFF60C000` | 1, 3 |
| repeat | mirror | `0xFF609000` | 1, 2 |
| clamp | repeat | `0xFFC06000` | 3, 1 |
| clamp | clamp | `0xFFC0C000` | 3, 3 |
| clamp | mirror | `0xFFC09000` | 3, 2 |
| mirror | repeat | `0xFF906000` | 2, 1 |
| mirror | clamp | `0xFF90C000` | 2, 3 |
| mirror | mirror | `0xFF909000` | 2, 2 |

Each axis takes its own state, and the two axes' answers on one word are
independent: the value is exactly what the index arithmetic of that axis's state
predicts, in all nine cells. A single shared wrap state would have produced
three words, not nine.

The linear footprint at the edge is wrapped per axis too. At s = t = 1.0 the two
taps are texels 15 and 16, and the stored word is the average of the two
blocks' colours:

| wrap s | wrap t | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| repeat | repeat | `0xFF787800` | 0x78 is the average of block 3 (`0xC0`) and block 0 (`0x30`) |
| repeat | clamp | `0xFF78C000` | x averaged with the wrapped block 0, y clamped to block 3 |
| clamp | clamp | `0xFFC0C000` | both taps block 3 |
| clamp | mirror | `0xFFC0C000` | mirrored texel 16 is 15, also block 3 |
| mirror | mirror | `0xFFC0C000` | same |

At the low corner (s = t = 0.0, taps at -1 and 0) with t clamped: repeat reads
`0xFF783000` (the -1 tap wraps to block 3, the 0 tap is block 0) while clamp and
mirror both read `0xFF303000` (both taps block 0). So the edge and the corner
agree with the interior: the state decides which texel the out-of-range tap
becomes, per axis, and clamp and mirror differ only where the wrapped index
reaches a different texel than the clamped one.

### What this settles

On this device and setup:

* the nearest mip filter rounds a tie down at 0.5, 1.5 and 2.5 on a chain whose
  levels all differ, and does so identically on a 32x32 and a 64x64 chain;
* the level changes 1/64 above the half-integer, not at it: values up to
  `k + 0.5078125` keep level k and `k + 0.515625` takes level k+1;
* the two wrap axes are independent: all nine combinations of repeat, clamp and
  mirrored repeat give the word the per-axis arithmetic predicts;
* the linear filter's out-of-range tap follows its own axis's state, at the high
  edge and at the low corner, so clamp and mirrored repeat differ exactly where
  the wrapped index differs from the clamped one.

### What this does not settle

* **The 1/64 offset's cause.** Quantising the level to 1/32 with a half-up carry
  and rounding with the boundary moved 1/64 lower fit the same cells; nothing
  here separates them, and no value between the two descriptions' predictions
  was measured.
* **The tie at 3.5 and 4.5.** Level 4 is `0x02` on this chain, so 3.5 is
  readable, but it was not run; 4.5 would need a 64x64 chain and a sixth word.
* **The blending mip filter's wrap.** `nearest-mipmap-linear` blending across
  the wrap seam was not measured; section 19 covered its fraction only.
* **Wrap state for the mip chain's own levels.** Whether the wrap state applies
  when the level itself is a single texel (level 5 and above) is untested.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-tex-state` and `verify-tex-state` hold 181 rows each
(59 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`01cfc0ed992c...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.

## 22. ALU marker edge cases: slot 0, packet boundaries, three senders

`asm_characterize.alu_marks_edge.json` (27 variants), template `asm/alu_marks.in`
(packet 0 primes r2 = 0.25 and r3 = 0.75, packet 1 the instructions under test,
packet 2 the single store). The store is `rt1`, so the observed word is the
framebuffer pixel, and the layout is `[r3.hi][r3.lo][r2.hi][r2.lo]` for
`store rt1, r2, r3` and `[r1.hi][r1.lo][r0.hi][r0.lo]` for `store rt1, r0, r1`.
Values: 0.0625 = `0x10`, 0.25 = `0x40`, 0.3125 = `0x50`, 0.5 = `0x7F`,
0.8125 = `0xCF` (the store's one-lsb deficit on 0.8125), 0.875 = `0xDF`,
1.0 = `0xFF`.

Packet 0 doubles as the previous-packet sender for the cross-packet group, since
its slots are free apart from the two primes.

### The questions

Section 18 left five things open: where slot 0's `(other)` goes, whether a send
crosses a packet boundary, what three senders do, what several senders aimed at
one slot do, and whether a receiver's own result and the incoming values
interact any differently from the single-sender case.

### Slot 0 with `(other)`

Slot 0 writes 0.25 and carries the marker; the other slots write
`r3 = 0.0625 + accumulator`:

| variant | receiver | word | r3 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `s0o-recv1-acc` | slot 1, unmarked | `0x00100040` | 0.0625 |
| `s0o-recv2-acc` | slot 2, unmarked | `0x00100040` | 0.0625 |
| `s0o-recv2this-acc` | slot 2, `(this)` | `0x00100040` | 0.0625 |
| `s0o-recv3-acc` | slot 3, unmarked | `0x00100040` | 0.0625 |
| `s0o-alone-acc` | none | `0x00BF0040` | 0.75 (prime) |

No slot receives it, including slot 2, which is what the `(i-1) & ~1` rule would
name for i = 0. Marking slot 2 `(this)` does not change its word either: it takes
slot 1's result, which is a NOP, so the send is still absent. The sender's own
destination keeps its bare 0.25 (`0x40`), and r3 keeps its prime, so the packet
ran with the marker set and nothing moved.

### Across a packet boundary

Sender in packet 0, receiver in packet 1:

| variant | send | receiver | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `xp-slot3-to-2-acc` | slot 3, 0.25 | slot 2, unmarked | `0x00100040` | receiver 0.0625 only |
| `xp-slot3-to-2-this-acc` | slot 3, 0.25 | slot 2, `(this)` | `0x00100040` | takes packet 1's slot 1 (a NOP) |
| `xp-slot1-to-0-acc` | slot 1, 0.25 | slot 0, unmarked | `0x0010007F` | receiver 0.0625 only; **r2 = 0.5** |
| `xp-slot1-to-0-this-acc` | slot 1, 0.25 | slot 0, `(this)` | `0x0010007F` | same |
| `xp-nomark-acc` | slot 3, unmarked | slot 2, unmarked | `0x00100040` | control |
| `xp-both-packets-acc` | slots 3 then 0 | slot 2, unmarked | `0x00100040` | no delivery |

The sends do **not** cross the boundary. The `xp-slot1-to-0` cells are worth
naming for a second reason: r2 rises from its 0.25 prime to 0.5, because in
packet 0 the send from slot 1 lands on slot 0, whose instruction writes r2 - the
in-packet rule acting within the sending packet, which is exactly what would be
missing if the value had been carried forward instead. The receiver in packet 1
stays at its bare 0.0625 in every cell.

### Three senders

Senders in slots 0, 1 and 2 (0.5, 0.25 and 0.0625 into r0, r1 and r2) and a
receiver in slot 3 marked `(this)`:

| variant | `-acc` | `-dest` | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `three-none` | `0x00BF0040` | `0x00000000` | the primes only |
| `three-012` | `0x00200010` | `0x004000CF` | r3 = 0.125, r0 = 0.8125, r1 = 0.25 |
| `three-012-this0` | `0x00200010` | `0x004000DF` | r0 = 0.875 |

The receiver in slot 3 reads 0.125 = 0.0625 + 0.0625: it picks up slot 2's own
0.0625 either as its previous slot under `(this)` or as a send, and the two
cannot be separated in this cell. Slot 0's destination is the informative one: it
holds 0.5 + 0.25 + 0.0625 = **0.8125**, so both sends aimed at slot 0 arrived and
the slot's own result was added on top. Marking slot 0 `(this)` as well raises it
to 0.875, one more 0.0625 - slot 3's own result - so the two markers' effects
still add separately.

With the senders in slots 1, 2 and 3 (`three-123`, the receiver in slot 0):

| store | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `-acc` | `0x00100050` | r2 = 0.3125 = 0.25 + 0.0625, r3 = 0.0625 |
| `-dest` | `0x007F00CF` | r0 = 0.8125 = 0.0625 + 0.5 + 0.25, r1 = 0.5 |

Slot 2's own destination rose by slot 3's 0.0625 (the send aimed at slot 2), and
the unmarked receiver in slot 0 still accumulated both sends aimed at slot 0, so
"unmarked" is not the same as "nothing arrives". Four senders (`three-all`) leave
the primes as they were and are the positive control that a slot with no
receiver of its own still writes its own value.

### Several senders aimed at one slot

Receiver in slot 0 (writing r2 = 0.0625 + accumulator), senders in slots 1 and 2:

| variant | word | r2 | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `comp-none-acc` | `0x00BF0010` | 0.0625 | controls: senders unmarked, no sends |
| `comp-12-acc` | `0x00BF00CF` | 0.8125 | 0.0625 + 0.25 + 0.5: **both arrived** |
| `comp-12-this0-acc` | `0x00BF00CF` | 0.8125 | `(this)` adds slot 3 (a NOP): unchanged |
| `comp-123-this0-acc` | `0x001000DF` | 0.875 | slot 3's 0.0625 also added |
| `comp-12-0send-acc` | `0x00BF00FF` | 1.0 | 0.75 + 0.25 + 0.5 = 1.5, **saturated** |
| `comp-12-dest` | `0x007F0040` | - | r0 = 0.25, r1 = 0.5, senders unaffected |

So a receiver whose own slot is the sends' target sums every value sent to it,
and the sum saturates at 1.0 rather than wrapping (`0xFF` for 1.5). This is the
case section 18's two-sender group did not build: there the receiver sat
somewhere else and the sends landed on a different slot, so the receiver could
only take its previous slot's value. Both readings are true of different
arrangements: the slot's accumulator sums what is sent to it, and the `(this)`
marker separately pulls the previous slot's result.

### What this settles

On this device and setup:

* slot 0 marked `(other)` transfers **nowhere inside the packet**; the
  `(i-1) & ~1` rule does not describe it, and the target instruction's `(this)`
  marker does not change that;
* a send does **not** cross a packet boundary in either direction tested: an
  unmarked or `(this)`-marked receiver in the next packet keeps only its own
  value, while the sending packet's own slots still accumulate as usual;
* with three senders each send lands on `(i-1) & ~1` in the same packet, and a
  slot that is itself a sender accumulates the values sent to it, so a slot's
  destination can be the sum of three contributions (0.8125 measured, and 0.875
  when `(this)` adds its previous slot);
* several senders aimed at the receiver's own slot are all added by that
  receiver, and the sum saturates at 1.0;
* the two markers' effects remain separable when both fire on the same slot.

### What this does not settle

* **Where slot 0's send goes.** Nothing in the packet, nothing in the next
  packet, and the sender's own destination is unchanged. Whether the value is
  dropped or written somewhere unobserved (a lane this harness cannot store)
  cannot be told apart.
* **The mechanism.** `(i-1) & ~1` is a description fitted to the transfer set,
  and the slot-0 exception shows it is not the right description; no claim is
  made about the control word's internal fields.
* **More than one packet of senders.** Only a two-packet program was built, and
  the cross-packet group uses packet 0 as the sender because it is otherwise
  free.
* **Saturation forms.** Only one overflow was produced (1.5 -> 1.0); how the
  accumulator behaves with negative values, or with an fxp difference between
  operands, is untested.
* **Two receivers of one send.** Every send here had at most one candidate
  target slot.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-marks-edge` and `verify-alu-marks-edge` hold 85
rows each (27 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`5b67e52edf59...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.

## 23. PSEQ visibility: the flush boundary, write ordering and a padded tiled source

`asm_characterize.pseq_vis.json` (11 variants), the section 17 templates
(`asm/pseq_split_a.in` writes the tag, `asm/pseq_split_b.in` fetches and observes
through rt4) and a new `asm/pseq_split_bw.in`, which can store to the fetch
source on either side of its own fetch. Extra draws are generated into
`asm/vis/` by `make_pseq_vis.py --write-extras`.

The staging is section 17's: a genuine 64x64 target (`GRATE_SIZE=64`), draw A
writes `tag(x, y) = 0xBF9F <y+1> <x+1>` to the source and fetches nothing, draw B
preloads pattern B (`0x9F607F40`), fetches the source with the program's
destination pair, and stores the result to rt4, whose whole 64x64 region the
runner digests. The source and the observation surface are different surfaces in
every variant, so a word in rt4 is a fetched word.

### The questions

Section 17 flushed between the draws, because that is what the harness did. Three
things followed from that, and one new surface case:

* does a fetch in a later draw see the earlier draw's writes **before** any
  explicit flush, or only after one?
* what happens when the fetching draw also writes the source, on either side of
  its own fetch?
* does the identity map survive a **tiled** source allocated wider than the
  target, which section 17 could only do for a linear one?

### Measured

| variant | digest | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `flush-two-draw` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | flushed: the identity map (section 17's picture) |
| `flush-nofetch` | `0x8086DDC5` | control: pattern B everywhere |
| `noflush-two-draw` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | **one submission, no flush: the same identity map** |
| `noflush-nofetch` | `0x8086DDC5` | its control |
| `flush-changed` | `0x54FFDDC5` | A, then C, then B, each flushed: C's picture |
| `noflush-changed` | `0x54FFDDC5` | the three draws in one submission: still C |
| `prewrite` | `0x8086DDC5` | draw B writes B to the source, then fetches: reads B |
| `postwrite` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | draw B fetches, then writes B: reads A's tag |
| `tiled-pad` | `0xFDFE7DC5` | tiled source allocated 16 texels wider: identity |
| `tiled-pad-shift` | `0x80261DC5` | the same with draw A shifted 16 columns |
| `tiled-pad-nofetch` | `0x8086DDC5` | its control |

Three pairs carry the answers:

* `noflush-two-draw` equals `flush-two-draw` byte for byte, and
  `noflush-nofetch` equals `flush-nofetch`, so the fetch that shares the earlier
  draw's submission reads exactly what it reads after a flush. On this setup an
  explicit flush between the draws is **not** what makes the earlier draw
  visible: submission order within one job is enough. The control is what makes
  that statement safe - if the fetch had simply not run, the pair would still
  match, but `noflush-nofetch` shows a different word, so the fetch did run and
  found the tags;
* `noflush-changed` equals `flush-changed`, so the order of three draws in one
  submission is respected and the last write before the fetch is what the fetch
  reads, not the first;
* `prewrite` reads B and `postwrite` reads the tag. A store from the same
  fragment reaches a later fetch in that fragment (which is section 14's
  read-after-write, here with the earlier draw's tags underneath), and a store
  after the fetch does not disturb it. So a draw that also writes the source is
  ordered with its own fetch, not resolved at the end of the draw.

`tiled-pad` reproduces the identity map through a *tiled* rt3 allocated 16
texels wider than the target, and `tiled-pad-shift` reproduces the shifted
picture digit for digit (`0x80261DC5`, the same digest the linear padded source
gave in section 17). A tile layout's row stride is the tile row, so the extra
allocation width does not shear the map: the fetch follows the target's tile
layout, and the pad beside it is simply not addressed.

### What this settles

On this device and setup:

* a fetch in a later draw reads the earlier draw's writes with **no explicit
  flush between the draws**: the map is identical to the flushed case, and the
  no-fetch controls separate "found the data" from "the fetch did not run";
* with three draws in one submission the order is respected, so the fetch reads
  the write issued closest before it, not the first one;
* a store to the source from the fetching fragment is visible to a fetch that
  follows it and does not disturb a fetch that precedes it, so the ordering is
  per instruction rather than per draw;
* a tiled source allocated wider than the target still gives the identity map,
  so the row stride comes from the surface's tile layout and not from the
  allocation width.

### What this does not settle

* **The address source.** Nothing here separates "the position registers" from
  "the fragment's own target coordinate": both are the same pixel for every
  fragment this harness can draw, and the fetch word has no offset field, so the
  two cannot be made to disagree. What varies here - the base surface (index
  field), its pitch, its tile layout and its allocation - all leave the map the
  identity, which is a statement about the *composition* of the address, not
  about which register supplies the pixel;
* **Cross-job visibility.** Both draws share one submission in the no-flush
  cells. A fetch submitted in a *later job* while the first is still in flight,
  or a fetch after a partial flush, is untested;
* **A source that is also the observation target.** rt4 never doubles as the
  fetch source here;
* **Write-after-read hazards in general.** Only one store on each side of the
  fetch was built, with pattern B as the value;
* **The tiled pad's limits.** Only +16 texels on a 64x64 tiled surface was
  measured, and the region read back is 64x64, so a fetch past the surface's
  logical size is not covered.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-pseq-vis` and `verify-pseq-vis` hold 37 rows each
(11 variants, three repeats, and the four control runs), pinned at
`6c17fce4b848...`; re-pinning from the verify directory is byte-identical.

## 24. DW field groups: is there a write mask

### The questions

Section 8 records every DW bit that a *single-bit* flip changes and calls 6..9,
11..14 and 18..31 inert. Two questions survive that sweep:

1. does the word carry a write mask at all - a field that leaves some of the
   four output bytes unwritten;
2. do the remaining bits act when set as a group rather than one at a time.

The render-target class field is the reason the second question is not idle.
Flipping its bits one at a time suggested "bit 16 kills the write, bit 17 is a
don't-care"; the field is only sensible as a 2-bit value, where 0 and 2 write,
1 needs a depth surface bound and 3 is invalid. A mask or a mode field in the
"inert" bits would fail the same way under single-bit flips.

### Why a prefill

A store that is dropped and a store that writes the value the target already
holds are the same readback on a cleared surface. Every variant therefore stores
a prefill pattern in batch 0 and the word under test in batch 1, both to rt1:

| batch | ALU writes | store |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0 | `r0/r1` = 17/256, 34/256, 51/256, 68/256 | `store rt1, r0, r1` |
| 1 | `r2/r3` = 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0 | the word under test |

The prefill stores as `0x44332211` and the payload as `0xFFBF7F40`, so a byte
that survives is a positive observation of "not written", and the four bytes
are distinct on both sides.

### Measured

Seventeen variants, three repeats, all agreeing; `store rt1, r2, r3` is
`0x00028005` (index 1, class 2, pair r2/r3):

| variant | word | stored | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `prefill-only` | NOP | `0x44332211` | batch 0's store is what the target holds |
| `all` | `0x00028005` | `0xFFBF7F40` | all four bytes replaced |
| `group-6-9` | `0x000283C5` | `0xFFBF7F40` | no mask in 6..9 |
| `group-18-19` | `0x000E8005` | `0xFFBF7F40` | the compiler's operand slices, as a group |
| `group-23-25` | `0x03828005` | `0xFFBF7F40` | ditto, plus bit 24 |
| `group-18-31` | `0xFFFE8005` | `0xFFBF7F40` | every bit above the class field |
| `group-11-14` | `0x0002F805` | `0x00000000` | a *zero payload*, not a masked write |
| `bit-11` | `0x00028805` | `0xFFBF7F40` | inert alone |
| `bit-12` | `0x00029005` | `0xFFBF7F40` | inert alone |
| `bit-13` | `0x0002A005` | `0xFFBF7F40` | inert alone |
| `bit-14` | `0x0002C005` | `0x00000000` | the group's zero is bit 14 |
| `bit-14-15` | `0x0002E005` | `0x00000000` | the pair bit does not change it |
| `bit-14-pair0` | `0x00024005` | `0x00000000` | neither does naming the other pair |
| `class-1` | `0x00018005` | `0x44332211` | no depth surface bound: the store is dropped |
| `mode-0` | `0x00028004` | `0x44332211` | nothing written |
| `mode-2` | `0x00028006` | `0x44332211` | spill write of one register: colour untouched |
| `mode-3` | `0x00028007` | `0x44332211` | spill write of several: colour untouched |

### What this settles

* **There is no per-byte write mask in the DW word on the colour path.** Bits
  6..9, 18..19, 23..25 and 18..31 together leave the full payload written, and
  the prefill makes that a statement about writing rather than about a cleared
  target. Any masking that exists is outside the instruction word (the blob's
  context carries a `write_mask` for its own bookkeeping).
* **Bits 11..13 are inert alone and bit 14 is not.** The group 11..14 only
  looked interesting because it contains 14.
* **Bit 14 zeroes the payload and still commits the write.** It is not a
  disable: the target moves from the prefill to `0x00000000`. It is also not
  the pair select - naming either pair gives the same zeros.
* **Modes 0, 2 and 3 and a class-1 store without a depth surface write nothing
  to the colour target**, now measured against a prefill instead of against the
  clear colour.

### What this does not settle

* where bit 14's zero comes from. "A source that reads zero" and "a deliberate
  clear-store" are indistinguishable from outside the unit, and the obvious
  candidate - the second row of row registers, `r4..r7` - cannot be populated:
  `alu_buffer_size = 2` hangs the draw even for a one-batch program whose whole
  body is one `MAD` and a store, with no result and no error. That is the same
  blocker [[Compiler Internals]] records for its own bit-14 attempt, now
  measured rather than inferred;
* the spill half-word index. Section 12.9 leaves bits 15 and 17 "open above
  bit 14" because each landed where the packet index did not predict, and
  settling it needs the raw spill surface, which this harness reports only as a
  region digest.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-dw-fields` and `verify-dw-fields` hold 55 rows
each (17 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`f0bbe8c07e39...`; re-pinning from the verification directory reproduces the
manifest byte for byte. Regenerate with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_dw_fields.py`.

## 25. The PSEQ form bit 24 selects

### The question

Section 9 says "bit 24 with bit 23 set selects the spill-fetch form, whose write
mask and slot fields overlay the low bits of this word (section 9.2)" - and
there is no 9.2. What is measured there is only that setting bit 24 on a working
fetch suppresses it, and that bits 4..15 are inert in the render-target form.

### The round trip

| batch | what it does |
|:--|:--|
| 0 | prime `r0/r1` with the "did nothing" pattern |
| 1 | prime `r2/r3` with the tag and `store rt4, r2, r3` |
| 2 | the word under test in the `PSEQ` slot |
| 3 | an optional second fetch, used to read rt4 back |
| 4 | `store rt1, r0, r1` |

A fetch replaces the pattern (`0x20102010`) with the tag (`0x8F706050`); a word
that does nothing leaves the pattern. The tag is stored through rt4 bound with
`GRATE_RT4=1`.

### Measured

Twenty variants, three repeats, all agreeing:

| variant | word | stored | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `rt4-fetch` | `0x00840008` | `0x8F706050` | the render-target form works |
| `nofetch` | NOP | `0x20102010` | the pattern |
| `rt4-index0` | `0x00800008` | `0x2010FFFF` | index 0: `r0` becomes `FFFF`, `r1` keeps its pattern |
| `spill-24` | `0x01000008` | `0x20100010` | `r0.hi` zeroed, no fetch |
| `spill-24-23` | `0x01800008` | `0x00102010` | `r1.hi` zeroed instead |
| `spill-b0` | `0x01000009` | `0x00102010` | bit 0 selects the same half as bit 23 |
| `spill-b2` | `0x0100000C` | `0x20100000` | both halves of `r0` zeroed |
| `spill-b1` | `0x0100000A` | `0x20102010` | nothing written |
| `spill-b4` | `0x01800018` | `0x00102010` | bits 4..15 add nothing |
| `spill-b12` | `0x01809008` | `0x00102010` | " |
| `spill-b15` | `0x01888008` | `0x00102010` | " |
| `spill-low` | `0x0180FFF8` | `0x00102010` | every bit 4..15 at once |
| `spill-nolow` | `0x01000000` | `0x20102010` | without bit 3 nothing is written |
| `spill-b23-nolow` | `0x01800000` | `0x20102010` | " |
| `spill-b2-nolow` | `0x01000004` | `0x20102000` | bit 2 alone writes `r0.lo` |
| `spill-b0-nolow` | `0x01000001` | `0x20102010` | bit 0 alone writes nothing |
| `spill-pair` | `0x0180000A` (read `r2/r3`) | `0x8F706050` | the pair bit writes nothing to `r2/r3` either |
| `spill-24-then-fetch` | `0x01000008`, then `0x00840008` | `0x8F706050` | the tag is still there |
| `spill-24-23-then-fetch` | `0x01800008`, then `0x00840008` | `0x8F706050` | " |
| `tag-then-fetch` | NOP, then `0x00840008` | `0x8F706050` | the readback control |

Two of those rows were first read the wrong way round. `store rt1, r0, r1`
stores `[r1.hi][r1.lo][r0.hi][r0.lo]`, so `0x20100000` is `r0.lo` and `r0.hi`,
not "both high halves"; `0x20102000` is `r0.lo`, not `r1.hi`. With this
template priming `r0` and `r1` with the *same* pair, a byte position could not
name a register either way, so neither reading could be checked from the word
alone. The grid below is the same question asked again with all four halves
primed differently.

### The destination grid

The bits that matter are 3, 2, 0 and 23. Sixteen combinations, five repeats
each; `r0` primed `(0x11, 0x22)` and `r1` primed `(0x33, 0x44)`, so the idle
readback is `0x44332211` and every written byte is visible in place:

| bits beside 24 | word | bytes written |
|:--|--:|:--|
| none | `0x01000000` | none |
| 0 | `0x01000001` | none |
| 23 | `0x01800000` | none |
| 3 | `0x01000008` | `r0.hi` |
| 2 | `0x01000004` | `r0.lo` |
| 3, 2 | `0x0100000C` | `r0.lo`, `r0.hi` |
| 0, 23 | `0x01800001` | `r0.lo` |
| 2, 0 | `0x01000005` | `r1.lo` |
| 2, 23 | `0x01800004` | `r1.lo` |
| 3, 0 | `0x01000009` | `r1.hi` |
| 3, 23 | `0x01800008` | `r1.hi` |
| 2, 0, 23 | `0x01800005` | `r0.lo`, `r1.lo` |
| 3, 0, 23 | `0x01800009` | `r0.lo`, `r1.hi` |
| 3, 2, 0 | `0x0100000D` | `r1.lo`, `r1.hi` |
| 3, 2, 23 | `0x0180000C` | `r1.lo`, `r1.hi` |
| 3, 2, 0, 23 | `0x0180000D` | `r0.lo`, `r1.lo`, `r1.hi` |

The sixteen rows have one reading. Bit 3 writes `r0.hi` and bit 2 writes
`r0.lo`, independently of each other; with **exactly one** of bits 0 / 23 set,
the same bytes of `r1` are written instead of `r0`; with **both** set, the `r1`
write happens *and* `r0.lo` is written as well. Every row above follows from
that, including the four both-set rows, whose extra `r0.lo` write is measured
but not explained.

What the grid rules out:

* that bits 0 and 23 are the same bit duplicated in the word's two halves. A
  duplicated bit would make `{0,23}` behave like `{0}`, and it writes where
  neither writes alone;
* that either bit means "the second half". Each moves the whole write from
  `r0` to `r1`, and neither writes a half on its own;
* that the low halves are untouched. `{2}` writes `r0.lo`, `{3,2}` writes both
  halves of `r0`, and ten of the sixteen rows write a low byte.

### The value written is not a constant zero

Every one of the eighty draws wrote the same bytes, but not all of them read
zero. In the recorded verification run three draws did not:

| variant | expected | draw |
|:--|--:|--:|
| `sel-b2-b0` (2, 0) | `0x44002211` | `0x44502211` |
| `sel-b2-b23` (2, 23) | `0x44002211` | `0x44502211` |
| `sel-b3-b2-b23` (3, 2, 23) | `0x00002211` | `0x00502211` |

Every dissent is a low byte holding `0x50` where the grid says zero, and every
dissenting cell is one that writes a low byte; other runs of the same manifest
dissented in one to five draws of eighty, in cells writing `r0.lo`, `r1.lo` or
both, with the same `0x50`. `0x50` is the prime of the tag
batch's `r2.lo` and also the low byte of the stored tag, so which of the two the
byte comes from is not separated. It is not the byte's own prime (`0x11` or
`0x33`), so the write happened and landed something else.

The remaining draws read plain zeros, and the same destination bytes read zeros
in a second program shape: `pseq_spill.in` (five batches, identical primes)
returns `0x00002010` for `{3,2,0}` and `{3,2,23}` in ten of ten draws of the
pinning run and fourteen of fourteen of the verification run. So the zero is
reproducible across shapes and the `0x50` is the exceptional case, not the rule.

So the destination map is stable and the written *value* is not: the form
writes a byte that reads back as zero in almost every draw, but it is a value
it produces rather than a constant `0.0`, and where that value comes from is
open.

### What this settles

* **The form is not a fetch.** No variant with bit 24 set delivers any part of
  the tagged surface; what it does instead is write selected bytes of the
  destination pair, high and low alike, so it is a byte map and not the
  high-half clear the earlier eight-cell table described.
* **The selection of bytes is in bits 3, 2, 0 and 23, not in bits 4..15.**
  Those are inert even as a group. Bit 3 writes `r0.hi`, bit 2 writes `r0.lo`,
  and bits 0 / 23 choose between the `r0` pair and the `r1` pair - with both
  set they write both pairs' bytes plus `r0.lo` again. The grid above is the
  whole sixteen-cell map; nothing in it establishes a rule for bits 4..15,
  other words, or other instruction slots.
* **Bit 1 suppresses it.** With bit 1 set nothing is written to `r0/r1` or to
  `r2/r3`, so in this form the destination-pair bit does not select a pair.
* **The form does not write the surface it would read.** A render-target fetch
  of rt4 after the word still returns the tag, so nothing was committed there
  either.

### What this does not settle

* how the written value is produced. It reads back as zero in almost every
  draw and as `0x50` in a few (see "The value written is not a constant zero").
  The
  surface held a tag in every word a fetch can reach, so "read from somewhere
  else" and "clear the register, occasionally with stale data" are both open;
* why the both-set rows write `r0.lo` a second time, and whether that is one
  write per set selector bit rather than a second destination;
* whether the map holds for bits above 23 or for destination registers other
  than `r0/r1` - neither was varied here;
* what a consumer would use a zero-half write for. A spill/restore sequence at a
  chunk boundary is the plausible context, and nothing here tests it;
* why the index-0 fetch writes `FFFF` into `r0` and leaves `r1` alone. It is
  recorded because it is repeatable and it means index 0 is not a plain colour
  read, but it is not part of this question.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-pseq-spill` and `verify-pseq-spill` hold 64 rows
each (20 variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`3d1dc560d30c...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_pseq_spill.py`.

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-pseq-spill-sel`, `verify-pseq-spill-sel` and
`clean-pseq-spill-sel` hold the grid: 16 variants at five repeats (80 rows, plus
the control rows), pinned from `4ef484b64084...` on the modal value of each cell.
A rerun of a cell that dissents fails that repeat against the pinned word, with
the other value in the `result` column of `results.tsv`; the generator prints
the dissent while pinning but does not write it into the manifest, which has to
re-pin byte for byte from a run that does not dissent.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_pseq_spill_sel.py`.

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-pseq-spill-value` and `verify-pseq-spill-value`
hold the two-word check against the older shape, 14 rows, pinned from
`--set value` of the same generator.

## 26. Which vertex an interpolation-disabled component takes

### The question

Section 10 measured that `fp20 (dis)` replaces a component's interpolated value
with "a vertex value" and recorded that which vertex it is was not determined
by the single sample. The modifier sets the per-column disable bit of the link
word, so the question is what the fragment sees for that column.

### Method

The vertex shader exports `a[1].zyxx`, so the harness's unit-vector vertex
colours become `(0,0,1,1)` for vertices 0 and 3, `(0,1,0,0)` for vertex 1 and
`(1,0,0,0)` for vertex 2. Three reference variants store each of those vectors
through the same destination bytes and the same store word as the varying
variant, so a `(dis)` word can be matched to a vertex by comparison instead of
by reasoning about the slot-to-component mapping:

| reference | vector | word |
|:--|:--|--:|
| `ref-v0v3` | (0, 0, 1, 1) | `0xFFFF0000` |
| `ref-v1` | (0, 1, 0, 0) | `0x000000FF` |
| `ref-v2` | (1, 0, 0, 0) | `0x0000FF00` |

### Measured

Eight variants, three repeats, all agreeing:

| variant | linker | stored | reading |
|:--|:--|--:|:--|
| `plain` | no modifier | `0x0000FF01` | interpolated, essentially vertex 2's vector |
| `dis-all` | `(dis)` on all four columns | `0xFFFF0000` | **exactly `ref-v0v3`** |
| `dis-x` | first column | `0x000000FF` | exactly `ref-v1` |
| `dis-y` | second column | `0x00000100` | no reference match |
| `dis-z` | third column | `0x00FF01FF` | no reference match |

### What this settles

* **With every column disabled the sample is one vertex's whole vector, and
  that vertex is the last one of the covering triangle.** The first fragment's
  triangle is (1,2,3), so `ref-v0v3` can only be vertex 3: the flat value is
  the provoking (last) vertex's, not the first's and not a blend;
* the interpolated control confirms the sample geometry: the plain word is
  vertex 2's vector to within one step in one component, and vertex 2 is in
  that triangle.

### What this does not settle

* the single-column words match no reference vector, so this set does not
  establish the per-component behaviour. `dis-x` happens to equal `ref-v1`
  exactly and `dis-z` differs from `plain` in two bytes, which is not what a
  single replaced component would look like under this byte layout - the ipl
  slot order and the link column order are not pinned against each other here.
  Section 10's own single-column result (blue replaced, alpha unchanged) stands
  as the only measurement on that side, and it used a different link word;
* whether the provoking-vertex rule survives a change of triangle order or a
  strip; only the quad's two triangles were run.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-mfu-dis` and `verify-mfu-dis` hold 28 rows each
(eight variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`fd756c64d60a...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_mfu_dis.py`.

## 27. Two writers into one register in the fp20 format

### The question

The forwarding set measured several writers into one half with `.l*` / `.*h`
destinations: the fx10 codes OR, order does not matter and two identical values
stay one value. It left open what an `.lh` (fp20) destination does with two
writers, and what happens when one writer uses each format.

### Method

Batch 0 runs two or three writers into `r2` and primes `r3` with 0.75 as a
marker. Batch 1 reads `r2` as fp20 and writes the fx10 conversion into `r2.lo`
(and `r3` into `r2.hi`), so the store's byte 0 is the collided value. The fp20
codes involved are `0.25 = 0xE8`, `0.5 = 0xF0`, `0.75 = 0xF4`, `1.0 = 0xF8`
and `1.5 = 0xFC`.

### Measured

Seven variants, three repeats, all agreeing:

| variant | writers | word | byte 0 | reading |
|:--|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `lh-one` | 0.25 | `0x00BF0040` | `0x40` | the baseline: 0.25 |
| `lh-same` | 0.25, 0.25 | `0x00BF0040` | `0x40` | identical values do not combine |
| `lh-two` | 0.25, 0.5 | `0x00BF00FF` | `0xFF` | **1.0**, not 0.75 |
| `lh-rev` | 0.5, 0.25 | `0x00BF00FF` | `0xFF` | order does not matter |
| `lh-three` | 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 | `0x00BF00FF` | `0xFF` | 1.5, saturated in the store |
| `fx10-then-fp20` | `.l*` 0.75, `.lh` 0.25 | `0x00BF0042` | `0x42` | 0.258 - neither value |
| `fp20-then-fx10` | `.lh` 0.25, `.l*` 0.75 | `0x00BF0042` | `0x42` | the same, either order |

### What this settles

* **The merge is a bitwise OR of the destination half's own code, and it does
  not care which format wrote it.** In fp20 `0xE8 | 0xF0 = 0xF8`, which is
  exactly 1.0 - that is the `0xFF` byte, not a clamp of 0.75. Three writers give
  `0xFC` = 1.5, which the 8-bit store then saturates;
* **identical values are idempotent**, the same as in the fx10 case;
* **mixing the formats leaves a value neither writer wrote** (`0x42`, one fp20
  step above 0.25), in both orders. A compiler cannot use one register as two
  scratch values in one packet, and a probe cannot assume the later writer wins.

### What this does not settle

* what writes `alu0..3` directly. The notes' own earlier attempt stands: a
  64-bit literal in an ALU slot is not a standalone instruction - it is taken as
  the slot word plus the next slot's control word - and the grammar refuses
  `aluN` as a destination, so no test in this set writes the latch register.
  What section 9 measures is that a row-register write is not relayed through
  the latches, not what would fill them;
* whether the OR is a property of the row-register file or of the ALU result
  merge. The behaviour is fixed; the stage that causes it is not;
* several writers into *different* halves of one register across several packets
  in one batch.

### Recorded runs

`tests/grate/asm/results/pin-alu-collide` and `verify-alu-collide` hold 25 rows
each (seven variants, three repeats, plus the four control rows), pinned from
`d9215e6560bb...`; re-pinning reproduces the manifest byte for byte.
Regenerate with `python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_collide.py`.

## 28. ALU result registers: what a read returns, and what writes them

`--set latch` (40 variants) from `tests/grate/asm/alu_latch.in`, manifest
`asm_characterize.alu_latch.json`. Six batches of four scalar slots with one DW
per batch; a variant fills only the slots it needs and leaves the rest NOP.

Section 9 concluded "ALU result registers read zero" and listed "what writes
`alu0..3`, if anything in the fragment ISA does" as open. Both readings are
wrong, and the old zero is explained by the shapes it was measured in: a
`aluK` read written *bare* (the fp20 form) from a packet whose slot K wrote
`.l*`, at a distance of a whole batch.

### The distance is the variable section 9 did not have

`alu_buffer_size` decides what a batch boundary is. At size 1 every batch is
its own group, so two batches are separated by a group flush. At size 2 two
batches share a group, so a producer in batch 0 and a reader in batch 1 differ
by a packet boundary only. At size 3 the group is three batches, so a reader
can sit two packets after the producer.

A store only produces a word in a group's first batch (section 3), so the
reader's output is stored by the next group: readers in batch 1, store in batch
2 at size 2, and the `*-direct` controls (a plain row write read back at the
same distance) show that path works.

### Same packet

Bytes are `r0.lo`, `r0.hi`, `r1.lo`, `r1.hi` from the least significant end;
the producer value is `0.0625` = `0x10` unless the row says otherwise.

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `same-chain` | `0x10100010` | three links: `r0.lo`, `r1.lo` and `r1.hi` all `0x10`, each slot reading the one before it |
| `same-chain-hi` | `0x00001010` | the middle link wrote `.*h`, so the link after it read 0 |
| `same-backward` | `0x00000040` | slot 0 read `alu1` and got `0.25`, which slot 1 wrote *later* in the packet |
| `same-two-values` | `0x00000010` | `alu0` = `0x10`; `alu1` read 0 because its writer used `.*h` |
| `same-self` | `0x00000000` | slot 2 reading `alu2`, its own latch, while writing it |
| `same-nop` | `0x00000000` | the latch of a slot the packet leaves NOP |
| `same-bare` | `0x00000000` | the same read written bare (`alu0`) instead of `alu0.l` |
| `same-bare-lh` | `0x00000010` | the bare read with an `.lh` (fp20) producer |
| `same-row`, `same-row-baseline` | `0x00000000` | a row read in the writing packet: no forwarding |

So inside a packet a latch read is served by the packet's own slot-K result,
in either slot order, and the format decides what is visible: `.l` sees a `.l*`
write, the bare fp20 read sees an `.lh` write and misses a `.l*` one. A slot
reading its own latch sees nothing.

### One packet later, same group (`alu_buffer_size` 2)

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `pkt-alu0-s0` | `0x00000010` | `alu0` from slot 0 |
| `pkt-alu0-s1` | `0x00000010` | `alu0` from slot 1 - the carry does not need slot 0 here |
| `pkt-alu1-s0` | `0x00000040` | `alu1` from slot 0 |
| `pkt-alu2-s0` | `0x0000007F` | `alu2` from slot 0 |
| `pkt-alu3-s0` | `0x00000020` | `alu3` from slot 0 |
| `pkt-two-latches` | `0x00007F40` | `alu1` read from slot 0 and `alu2` from slot 1 at once |
| `pkt-alu0-hi` | `0x00000000` | the producer wrote `.*h` only |
| `pkt-alu0-fp20` | `0x00000000` | the producer wrote `.lh` and the reader asked for `.l` |
| `pkt-row` | `0x00000000` | a row read one packet later: not visible inside the group |
| `pkt-direct` | `0x00000040` | the same row read two batches later, across the group boundary |
| `pkt-bare` | `0x00000000` | the bare read one packet later |

All four latches survive one packet boundary, from any reading slot, and only
the low-half (`.l*`) writes fill the fx10 view. The row register does not: it
becomes visible at the group boundary instead.

### Two packets later and across a batch

| variant | size | word | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|:--|
| `pkt3-alu0-s0`, `pkt3-alu1-s0` | 3 | `0x00000000` | a latch does not survive two packet boundaries inside a group |
| `n1-alu0-s0` | 1 | `0x00000010` | `alu0` read from slot 0 across the group flush |
| `n1-alu0-s1` | 1 | `0x00000000` | the same latch from slot 1 |
| `n1-alu1-s0` | 1 | `0x00000000` | `alu1` across the flush |
| `n1-row` | 1 | `0x00000010` | a row write across the flush: committed |
| `n1-direct` | 1 | `0x00000040` | the same distance without any latch involved |

So one value crosses a group flush: `alu0` read from slot 0. That is exactly
the shape section 9 measured, and exactly why it saw zeros everywhere else.
