same-run comparison meaningful.

**The "4" row of the distribution table cannot be measured.** Four packets in
one batch at `alu_buffer_size` 1 is not encodable: `src/libgrate/fragment_asm.y`
raises "Over 3 ALU instructions per exec batch" from the `ALU_INSTRUCTION`
action, and the count field of `0x801` only encodes 1 to 3. `asm/alu_blocks4one.in`
is kept as the preserved probe; the assembler exits 1 with

    fs: line 33: Over 3 ALU instructions per exec batch
    /tmp/blocks4one.in assembler parse failed

so that row is an assembler limitation, not a hardware result, and the tested
four-packet arrangements are the six in the table above.

Manifests `asm_characterize.alu_dist211.json`, `..._dist121.json`,
`..._dist112.json` and `..._blocks4.json` (56 variants each) from
`make_alu_m4dist.py --set dist211|dist121|dist112|blocks4`: 49 matrix variants
built by loops rather than typed by hand (12 forward cells, 2 reading-slot
re-reads, 16 wrap cells, 4 own-packet, 4 two-apart, 3 backward, 3 two-writer,
5 baselines) and the same 7 cross-shape controls in every manifest.

### The forward matrix holds in all three shapes

| read | 2 + 1 + 1 | 1 + 2 + 1 | 1 + 1 + 2 |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|
| `fwd01-al0` .. `al3` | `0x10` | `0x10` | `0x10` |
| `fwd12-al0` .. `al3` | `0x20` | `0x20` | `0x20` |
| `fwd23-al0` .. `al3` | `0x40` | `0x40` | `0x40` |
| `fwd01-al0-s2`, `fwd23-al3-s2` | `0x10`, `0x40` | `0x10`, `0x40` | `0x10`, `0x40` |

Thirty-six cells, every one naming the packet that wrote. The forward half of
the map does not depend on the number of batches, on the position of the
multi-packet batch, or on the latch.

### The wrap matrix is complete in all three shapes

| wrap | 2 + 1 + 1 | 1 + 2 + 1 | 1 + 1 + 2 |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|
| `wrap-al0-s0` .. `-s3` | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 |
| `wrap-al1-s0` .. `-s3` | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 |
| `wrap-al2-s0` .. `-s3` | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 |
| `wrap-al3-s0` .. `-s3` | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 | `0x60` x4 |

Forty-eight cells: packet 3's slot-L write reaches packet 0's aluL read for
every latch and every reading slot, in every three-batch distribution. The
1 + 1 + 2 shape is the one that matters most for the earlier reading of the
anomaly: there packet 3 shares its batch with packet 2, the local arrangement
it has in 2 + 2, and the wrap still works.

### Negatives and conflicts

| variant | 2 + 1 + 1 | 1 + 2 + 1 | 1 + 1 + 2 | reading |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|:--|
| `own-p0` .. `own-p3` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | a packet never sees its own result |
| `pair-p0-p2`, `pair-p2-p0`, `pair-p1-p3`, `pair-p3-p1` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | two apart is not the predecessor, either direction |
| `back-p1-p0`, `back-p2-p1`, `back-p3-p2` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | a later packet does not serve an earlier one |
| `both-1-2-read3` | `0x40` | `0x40` | `0x40` | of two writers with the reader after both, the predecessor supplies the read |
| `both-2-3-read0` | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` | the wrap's writer wins over the packet before it |
| `both-0-1-read3` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | packets 0 and 1 are not packet 3's predecessor |
| `write-only-p3` | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` | the write itself lands, stored from the row |
| `read-nowrite` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | no writer, no value |
| `unmarked`, `none` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | baselines |
| `row-direct` | `0x10` | `0x10` | `0x10` | the row path reaches the store |

`both-0-1-read3` is the one that rules out a "last writer in the group wins"
reading for this shape: two packets write the latch, and packet 3 - whose
predecessor is packet 2 - still reads zero.

### The same-run controls

| control | word | meaning |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `ctl-rot2` | `0x40` | the known-good size-2 two-packet rotation |
| `ctl-rot3` | `0x7F` | the known-good size-3 three-packet rotation |
| `ctl-carry-s1` | `0x10` | the group-boundary carry that does work |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap0` | `0x00` | 2 + 2's latch-0 wrap, still missing |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap3` | `0x60` | 2 + 2's latch-3 wrap, still present |
| `ctl-ref31-wrap0` | `0x60` | 3 + 1's latch-0 wrap, still present |
| `ctl-ref31-fwd01` | `0x10` | 3 + 1's adjacent hop |

All seven carry the same words in all four runs (four manifests, two passes
each, plus the clean runs below), so each manifest reproduces the earlier
sections' results alongside its own.

### The size-4 reference with all four latches

Section 38 measured the size-4, one-packet-per-batch shape with a single
`ALU0` per batch, which established the cycle for latch 0 only.
`asm/alu_blocks4.in` keeps that grouping and gives each batch all four slots:
eight batches, two four-packet groups, the store in group B's first batch
(4). The same 49-variant matrix runs on it (`s4-` prefix):

| part | cells | result |
|:--|--:|:--|
| forward 0 -> 1, 1 -> 2, 2 -> 3 | 12 | `0x10`, `0x20`, `0x40` for every latch |
| wrap 3 -> 0 | 16 | `0x60` for every latch and reading slot |
| own-packet | 4 | `0x00` |
| two apart, both directions | 4 | `0x00` |
| one hop backwards | 3 | `0x00` |
| two writers | 3 | `0x40`, `0x60`, `0x00` |
| write only / no write / baselines | 5 | `0x60` / `0x00` / `0x00` / `0x00` / `0x10` |

`s4-wrap-al0-s0` reproduces section 38's `m4-s4-wrap` (`0x60`), and the other
forty-eight cells are new. The complete four-latch cycle at size 4 is therefore
measured, not inferred from the latch-0 case.

### What is measured, inferred and open

* **Measured**: at `alu_buffer_size` 3 with four packets split 2 + 1 + 1,
  1 + 2 + 1 or 1 + 1 + 2, the cyclic map is complete - twelve forward cells and
  sixteen wrap cells per shape, every negative and every control as expected.
  At `alu_buffer_size` 4 with one packet per batch the complete map holds for
  all four latches. The 2 + 2 shape keeps returning `0x00` for latches 0-2's
  wrap and `0x60` for latch 3's in the same runs.
* **Inferred**: the shortfall is not a property of four packets, of a
  multi-batch group, of the position of a multi-packet batch, of packet 0
  sharing its batch, or of packet 3 sharing its batch. Among the seven tested
  four-packet arrangements, 2 + 2 is the only one with the shortfall and the
  only one in which no batch holds a single packet; it is also the only one in
  which both wrap endpoints sit in multi-packet batches. Those three statements
  pick out the same shape in this data set, so the measurements select between
  "position" and "2 + 2-like", not between the three formulations of the
  latter.
* **Open**: which of the three 2 + 2-specific properties is the cause. A
  five-packet group would separate them: 1 + 2 + 2 has two multi-packet batches
  and a single-packet batch, 2 + 3 has two multi-packet batches and no
  single-packet batch, and 1 + 1 + 3 has a single-packet batch but no
  two-packet one. All three splits are encodable (a batch holds at most three
  packets, so 4 + 1 and 1 + 4 are not), but five-packet groups are untested.
  Also open: whether a two-packet batch at a different group size behaves like
  2 + 2; whether the slot-3 specialness of 2 + 2 is the same mechanism seen from
  the other side; and the hardware mechanism itself, which none of these runs
  observes.

### Recorded runs

`pin-alu-dist211` / `verify-alu-dist211`, `-dist121`, `-dist112` and
`-blocks4` hold 172 rows each (56 variants, three repeats, plus the two
controls before and after), with `schedule-words.tsv` from the run itself. The
pin runs were made on `5350c8372aa7021c988112d2ca6eff06972b2410` with the new
files untracked and no tracked file modified; the verify runs are the pinned
manifests, whose sha256 matches the checked-in manifests. Pin and verify agree
row for row in all four sets. `clean-alu-dist211` and the other three are the
committed manifests run from a fresh `git worktree` at the commit that contains
them, empty `git status` before and after, output directories outside the
checkout; all four match their pinned rows exactly. Regenerate with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_m4dist.py --set dist211|dist121|dist112|blocks4`
and pin with `--results DIR`.

## 44. Five-packet groups: simple batch-occupancy hypotheses do not reproduce the anomaly

Section 43 left the four-packet 2 + 2 shortfall narrowed to three properties that
2 + 2 shares and every other tested four-packet shape lacks: two multi-packet
batches, no single-packet batch, and both wrap endpoints in multi-packet
batches. All three name the same shape in four-packet data, so that data could
not separate them. A five-packet group can: `1 + 2 + 2` has two multi-packet
batches *and* a single-packet batch, `2 + 3` has two multi-packet batches and no
single-packet batch, and `2 + 1 + 2` has both wrap endpoints in multi-packet
batches with a single-packet batch elsewhere.

### The eight distributions

Five packets fit a two-batch group only as 2 + 3 or 3 + 2, and a three-batch
group in the six ways below; `asm/alu_m5_23.in` ... `asm/alu_m5_311.in` carry
them, one template per distribution, all at `alu_buffer_size` 2 or 3:

| set | split | size | group A batches | multi-packet batches | packet 0 multi? | packet 4 multi? |
|:--|:--|--:|:--|--:|:--|:--|
| 23 | 2 + 3 | 2 | packets 0,1 \| 2,3,4 | 2 | yes | yes |
| 32 | 3 + 2 | 2 | packets 0,1,2 \| 3,4 | 2 | yes | yes |
| 113 | 1 + 1 + 3 | 3 | packet 0 \| 1 \| 2,3,4 | 1 | no | yes |
| 122 | 1 + 2 + 2 | 3 | packet 0 \| 1,2 \| 3,4 | 2 | no | yes |
| 131 | 1 + 3 + 1 | 3 | packet 0 \| 1,2,3 \| 4 | 1 | no | no |
| 212 | 2 + 1 + 2 | 3 | packets 0,1 \| 2 \| 3,4 | 2 | yes | yes |
| 221 | 2 + 2 + 1 | 3 | packets 0,1 \| 2,3 \| 4 | 2 | yes | no |
| 311 | 3 + 1 + 1 | 3 | packets 0,1,2 \| 3 \| 4 | 1 | yes | no |

These are all eight legal distributions for groups of two or three batches
with five packets and no batch larger than three. Four-batch groups are also
encodable; the permutations of 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 at size 4 were not part of this
sweep. Each template keeps the five packets in group A, repeats the same
distribution in the complete group B, and puts the readback DW in group B's
first batch (batch 2 at size 2, batch 3 at size 3), so the program is two whole
groups with no padding: 4 or 6 batches, an exact multiple of the group size.

Testability limits, checked in the assembler rather than assumed: a batch holds
at most three `ALU` blocks (`src/libgrate/fragment_asm.y`, "Over 3 ALU
instructions per exec batch"), so `4 + 1` and `1 + 4` are not encodable; and
`alu_buffer_size` accepts 0..4, so the two- and three-batch groups above are the
only ones this sweep uses. The schedule address field is six bits, not three
(`instr_sched` in `src/libgrate/fragment_asm.h`), which is what lets a ten-packet
program keep ten distinct addresses; the run's own dump confirms addresses 0..9.

### Payloads

Packet *k* writes uniform `k`'s value: u0 = 0.0625 (0x10), u1 = 0.125 (0x20),
u2 = 0.25 (0x40), u3 = 0.375 (0x60), and u4 = 0.4375 (0x70), the fifth value
this sweep adds. Each manifest validates all five values in the same run with
`row-direct-p0` ... `row-direct-p4`, which write the payload in packet 0 and
store the row directly through r2: 0x10, 0x20, 0x40, 0x60, 0x70 in all eight
distributions. A conflict test never pairs two producers with the same value, so
every non-zero result names its source packet uniquely.

### Actual schedule words

From each run's raw dump (`schedule-words.tsv`: `count@address` per batch):

| set | batch words |
|:--|:--|
| 23 | `2@0`, `3@2`, `2@5`, `3@7` |
| 32 | `3@0`, `2@3`, `3@5`, `2@8` |
| 113 | `1@0`, `1@1`, `3@2`, `1@5`, `1@6`, `3@7` |
| 122 | `1@0`, `2@1`, `2@3`, `1@5`, `2@6`, `2@8` |
| 131 | `1@0`, `3@1`, `1@4`, `1@5`, `3@6`, `1@9` |
| 212 | `2@0`, `1@2`, `2@3`, `2@5`, `1@7`, `2@8` |
| 221 | `2@0`, `2@2`, `1@4`, `2@5`, `2@7`, `1@9` |
| 311 | `3@0`, `1@3`, `1@4`, `3@5`, `1@8`, `1@9` |

Every word's count and address match the intended split for both groups, no
`0x806` word is non-zero in any run, and the addresses reach 9 without a wrap,
which confirms the six-bit field rather than the three-bit reading of the
earlier notes. The assembler derives all of this from the block placement; no
schedule word is patched, and the numbers above are read back from the hardware
run rather than from the template.

### Forward edges

Sixteen cells per distribution (four edges x four latches), all measured, all
passing with the value of the source packet:

| set | 0 -> 1 | 1 -> 2 | 2 -> 3 | 3 -> 4 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 23 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 32 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 113 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 122 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 131 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 212 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 221 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |
| 311 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 |

`fwd01-al0-s2` and `fwd34-al3-s2` read the same two edges from a different
slot and return the same bytes, so the reading slot does not enter the forward
path at M = 5 either.

### The wrap from packet 4 to packet 0

Sixteen cells per distribution; packet 4 writes 0x70 through slot N to test
aluN, while packet 0's reading slot is varied independently:

| set | alu0 s0..s3 | alu1 s0..s3 | alu2 s0..s3 | alu3 s0..s3 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 23 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 32 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 113 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 122 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 131 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 212 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 221 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 311 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 | 0x70 x4 |

One hundred and twenty-eight cells, every one carrying packet 4's value. All
four slot-indexed latch writes reach packet 0 from every reader slot in every
distribution. The writer slot is coupled to the latch index in this matrix, so
other writer-slot/latch cross-combinations were not tested. No reader-slot
restriction was observed, including the two distributions where packet 4 shares
its batch with earlier packets and the two where packet 0 shares its batch with
a later packet. In particular `23` is the closest five-packet analogue of
2 + 2: same group size, two packets in the first batch, no single-packet batch,
both wrap endpoints in multi-packet batches - and its wrap is complete.

### Negatives and conflicts

In all eight distributions, from the same runs:

| variant | measured | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `own-p0` .. `own-p4` | 0x00 x5 | a packet never sees its own result |
| `succ-p*` (5 pairs) | 0x00 | the immediate successor does not serve a read |
| `twoahead-p*` (5 pairs) | 0x00 | two ahead does not serve a read |
| `twoback-p*` (5 pairs) | 0x00 | two behind does not serve a read |
| `both-1-2-read3` | 0x40 | packet 2, the predecessor, not packet 1 |
| `both-2-3-read4` | 0x60 | packet 3, the predecessor |
| `both-3-4-read0` | 0x70 | packet 4, the wrap's writer |
| `both-2-4-read0` | 0x70 | packet 4, not packet 2 |
| `both-0-4-read2` | 0x00 | neither writer is packet 1, packet 2's predecessor |
| `three-1-3-4-read0` | 0x70 | packet 4 of the last pair wins |
| `three-0-2-3-read4` | 0x60 | packet 3, the predecessor |
| `write-only-p4` | 0x70 | the write lands and the row path works |
| `read-nowrite` | 0x00 | no writer, no value |
| `unmarked` | 0x00 | five writes without a reader leave nothing |
| `none` | 0x00 | the word baseline |
| `row-direct-p0` .. `p4` | 0x10, 0x20, 0x40, 0x60, 0x70 | payload values are distinct and legal |

The fifteen non-predecessor pairs per distribution are the complete set: for
every reader, the three sources that are neither itself nor its predecessor,
which covers the immediate successor in both directions of the ring and the two
packets at distance two. All 120 cells are zero, so the rotation is exact in all
eight shapes and not an "any earlier packet in the group" rule: in `23`, for
example, packet 0 (batch 0) does not serve packet 4 (batch 1), even though it is
earlier in the group.

### Controls

Every run of every manifest carries the same controls and returns the same
words:

| control | word | meaning |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `ctl-rot2` | 0x40 | size-2 two-packet rotation, still working |
| `ctl-rot3` | 0x7F | size-3 three-packet rotation, still working |
| `ctl-carry-s1` | 0x10 | size-1 group-boundary carry |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap0` | 0x00 | the 2 + 2 latch-0 wrap, still missing in the same run |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap3` | 0x60 | the 2 + 2 latch-3 wrap, still present |
| `ctl-ref31-wrap0` | 0x60 | the 3 + 1 wrap, complete |
| `ctl-ref31-fwd01` | 0x10 | the 3 + 1 adjacent hop |
| `ctl-ref211-wrap0` | 0x60 | the size-3 2 + 1 + 1 wrap, complete |

The two `immediate-operand` and `varying-pipeline` harness controls ran before
and after every sweep and returned `0x000000FF` and `0xFF01FF00`.

### Reproducibility

Manifests `asm_characterize.alu_m5_23.json` ... `asm_characterize.alu_m5_311.json`,
78 variants each (70 matrix, 8 controls), from `make_alu_m5dist.py --set
23|32|113|122|131|212|221|311`; three repeats per variant, 238 rows per run
(234 variant draws plus four harness-control rows), and `schedule-words.tsv`
dumped from the run itself. Pin runs used the unpinned manifests; verify runs
used the pinned manifests, whose sha256 matches the checked-in ones; pin and
verify agree row for row in all eight sets. `clean-alu-m5-*` are the committed
manifests run from a fresh `git worktree` at the commit that contains them, with
an empty `git status` before and after and the outputs outside the checkout; all
eight match their pinned rows exactly.

### What is measured, inferred and open

* **Measured**: in all eight five-packet distributions, the 16 forward cells,
  16 wrap cells, five own-packet negatives, fifteen non-predecessor pairs, seven
  conflict cases and five baselines behave exactly as the cyclic rule predicts
  for `0 -> 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 -> 0`, with the same words in every run.
* **Inferred**: the four-packet 2 + 2 shortfall is not caused by having two
  multi-packet batches (`23`, `32`, `122`, `212`, `221` have two and are
  complete), by the absence of a single-packet batch (`23` and `32` have none
  and are complete), or by both wrap endpoints sitting in multi-packet batches
  (`23`, `32` and `212` do and are complete). The tested batch-order
  reversals (`23`/`32`, `113`/`311`, `122`/`221`) agree; `131` and
  `212` are symmetric under reversal and also have complete cycles. The
  shortfall is confined to the tested four-packet group split 2 + 2; at M = 5
  no tested arrangement reproduces it.
* **Open**: whether "two packets in every batch" is the property, as opposed to
  something specific to four packets. The cheapest discriminator is a six-packet
  group split `2 + 2 + 2` at size 3 - uniform two per batch at a different packet
  count - against `3 + 3` and `1 + 2 + 3` at the same size. If `2 + 2 + 2` is
  restricted and the others are not, the property is uniform two-per-batch; if
  all three are complete, the shortfall is specific to four packets in two
  batches. Also open: whether `1 + 1 + 1 + 2` at size 4 (a four-batch group)
  behaves like the size-3 shapes, and the hardware mechanism, which none of
  these runs observes.

## 45. Six-packet groups: uniform two-packet batches do not reproduce the anomaly

Section 44 left one reading open. At M = 5 every tested split has the complete
cycle, including 2 + 3 (two multi-packet batches, no singleton), 1 + 2 + 2 (two
multi-packet batches with a singleton) and 2 + 1 + 2 (both wrap endpoints in
multi-packet batches). None of the four-packet 2 + 2 properties therefore
reproduced the shortfall at M = 5, which left either "four packets" or "two
packets in every batch" as the candidate. Six packets separate those readings:
2 + 2 + 2 has two packets in every batch at a different packet count, while
3 + 3 and 1 + 2 + 3 keep the packet count and change the occupancy. 3 + 2 + 1
is the batch-order reverse of 1 + 2 + 3.

Hypotheses:

| hypothesis | prediction |
|:--|:--|
| the shortfall follows uniform two-packet batches | 2 + 2 + 2 loses the wrap; 3 + 3 and 1 + 2 + 3 keep it |
| the shortfall needs three-packet batches | 2 + 2 + 2 keeps the complete map; 3 + 3 loses it |
| the shortfall needs four packets packed as two batches of two | all four keep the complete map |
| the shortfall follows batch order or wrap endpoint position | 1 + 2 + 3 and 3 + 2 + 1 differ |

### Shape, manifests, words

`asm/alu_m6_222.in`, `asm/alu_m6_33.in`, `asm/alu_m6_123.in` and
`asm/alu_m6_321.in` are whole two-group programs at `alu_buffer_size` 3, 2, 3
and 3. `222` is six batches of two packets, `33` is four batches of three,
`123` is 1 + 2 + 3 and `321` is 3 + 2 + 1, both over six batches. Group A holds
the six packets under test, group B repeats the same distribution, and the DW
in group B's first batch (3, 2, 3 and 3 respectively) stores group A's rows.
No schedule word is patched; the assembler derives each batch's count from its
`ALU:` blocks and each address from the running packet index.

| set | split | size | group A batches | multi-packet batches | two-packet batches |
|:--|:--|--:|:--|--:|--:|
| 222 | 2 + 2 + 2 | 3 | packets 0,1 \| 2,3 \| 4,5 | 3 | 3 |
| 33 | 3 + 3 | 2 | packets 0,1,2 \| 3,4,5 | 2 | 0 |
| 123 | 1 + 2 + 3 | 3 | packet 0 \| 1,2 \| 3,4,5 | 2 | 1 |
| 321 | 3 + 2 + 1 | 3 | packets 0,1,2 \| 3,4 \| 5 | 2 | 1 |

All four are encodable: a batch holds at most three `ALU` blocks
(`src/libgrate/fragment_asm.y`, "Over 3 ALU instructions per exec batch") and
`alu_buffer_size` accepts 0..4. The schedule address field is six bits, so a
twelve-packet program keeps twelve distinct addresses.

The actual words read back from each run's raw dump (`schedule-words.tsv`,
`count@address` per batch):

| set | batch words |
|:--|:--|
| 222 | `2@0`, `2@2`, `2@4`, `2@6`, `2@8`, `2@10` |
| 33 | `3@0`, `3@3`, `3@6`, `3@9` |
| 123 | `1@0`, `2@1`, `3@3`, `1@6`, `2@7`, `3@9` |
| 321 | `3@0`, `2@3`, `1@5`, `3@6`, `2@9`, `1@11` |

Every count and address matches the intended split for both groups, and every
`0x806` word is zero. The numbers above are the uploaded words, not the
template's intended shape.

### Payloads

Packet *k* writes uniform `k`'s value: u0 = 0.0625 (0x10), u1 = 0.125 (0x20),
u2 = 0.25 (0x40), u3 = 0.375 (0x60), u4 = 0.4375 (0x70), and u5 = 0.03125
(0x08), the sixth value this sweep adds. Each manifest validates all six values
in the same run with `row-direct-p0` ... `row-direct-p5`, which write the
payload in packet 0 and store the row directly through r2: 0x10, 0x20, 0x40,
0x60, 0x70, 0x08 in all four distributions. A conflict test never pairs two
producers with the same value, so every non-zero result names its source
packet uniquely.

### Forward edges

Twenty cells per distribution (five edges x four latches), all measured, all
carrying the source packet's byte:

| set | 0 -> 1 | 1 -> 2 | 2 -> 3 | 3 -> 4 | 4 -> 5 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 222 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 33 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 123 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 | 0x70 x4 |
| 321 | 0x10 x4 | 0x20 x4 | 0x40 x4 | 0x60 x4 | 0x70 x4 |

`fwd01-al0-s2` and `fwd45-al3-s2` read the first and last edges from another
slot and return `0x10` and `0x70`, so the reading slot does not enter the
forward path at M = 6 either.

### The wrap from packet 5 to packet 0

Sixteen cells per distribution; packet 5 writes 0x08 through slot N to test
aluN, while packet 0's reading slot is varied independently:

| set | alu0 s0..s3 | alu1 s0..s3 | alu2 s0..s3 | alu3 s0..s3 |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 222 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 |
| 33 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 |
| 123 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 |
| 321 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 | 0x08 x4 |

Sixty-four cells, every one carrying packet 5's value. All four latches reach
packet 0 from every reader slot in every distribution, including alu0. The
writer slot is coupled to the latch index in this matrix, so other
writer-slot/latch cross-combinations were not tested.

In particular `222` is the closest six-packet analogue of the M=4 2 + 2 shape:
the same uniform two-packet occupancy, with three batches instead of two. Its
wrap is complete, including the latch-0 wrap that the four-packet 2 + 2 shape
loses.

### Negatives and conflicts

In all four distributions, from the same runs:

| variant | measured | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `own-p0` .. `own-p5` | 0x00 x6 | a packet never sees its own result |
| `succ-p*` (6 pairs) | 0x00 | the immediate successor does not serve a read |
| `twoahead-p*` (6 pairs) | 0x00 | two ahead does not serve a read |
| `threeahead-p*` (6 pairs) | 0x00 | three ahead does not serve a read |
| `twoback-p*` (6 pairs) | 0x00 | two behind does not serve a read |
| `both-1-2-read3` | 0x40 | packet 2, the predecessor, not packet 1 |
| `both-2-3-read4` | 0x60 | packet 3, the predecessor |
| `both-3-4-read5` | 0x70 | packet 4, the predecessor |
| `both-4-5-read0` | 0x08 | packet 5, the wrap's writer |
| `both-1-5-read0` | 0x08 | packet 5 over an earlier writer |
| `both-2-5-read0` | 0x08 | packet 5 over a writer two packets back |
| `both-0-5-read2` | 0x00 | neither writer is packet 1, packet 2's predecessor |
| `three-1-3-5-read0` | 0x08 | packet 5 of the last pair wins |
| `three-0-2-4-read5` | 0x70 | packet 4, the predecessor |
| `write-only-p5` | 0x08 | the write lands and the row path works |
| `read-nowrite` | 0x00 | no writer, no value |
| `unmarked` | 0x00 | six writes without a reader leave nothing |
| `none` | 0x00 | the word baseline |
| `row-direct-p0` .. `p5` | 0x10, 0x20, 0x40, 0x60, 0x70, 0x08 | payload values are distinct and legal |

The twenty-four non-predecessor pairs per distribution are the complete set:
for every reader, the four sources that are neither itself nor its cyclic
predecessor. All 96 cells are zero, so the rotation is exact in all four shapes
and not an "any earlier packet in the group" rule: in `222`, for example,
packet 0 (batch 0) does not serve packet 5 (batch 2), even though it is earlier
in the group.

### Controls

Every run of every manifest carries the same ten controls and returns the same
words:

| control | word | meaning |
|:--|--:|:--|
| `ctl-rot2` | 0x40 | size-2 two-packet rotation, still working |
| `ctl-rot3` | 0x7F | size-3 three-packet rotation, still working |
| `ctl-carry-s1` | 0x10 | size-1 group-boundary carry |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap0` | 0x00 | the M=4 2 + 2 latch-0 wrap, still missing in the same run |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap3` | 0x60 | the M=4 2 + 2 latch-3 wrap, still present |
| `ctl-ref31-wrap0` | 0x60 | the M=4 3 + 1 wrap, complete |
| `ctl-ref31-fwd01` | 0x10 | the M=4 3 + 1 adjacent hop |
| `ctl-ref211-wrap0` | 0x60 | the M=4 2 + 1 + 1 wrap, complete |
| `ctl-ref5-wrap0` | 0x70 | the M=5 2 + 3 wrap, complete |
| `ctl-ref5-fwd34` | 0x60 | the M=5 3 -> 4 latch-3 hop |

The two `immediate-operand` and `varying-pipeline` harness controls ran before
and after every sweep and returned `0x000000FF` and `0xFF01FF00`. The
`ctl-ref22-*` pair is the same-run proof that the M=4 anomaly was still
present while the six-packet shapes were measured.

### Reproducibility

Manifests `asm_characterize.alu_m6_222.json`, `..._33.json`, `..._123.json` and
`..._321.json`, 97 variants each (87 matrix, 10 controls), from
`make_alu_m6dist.py --set 222|33|123|321`; three repeats per variant, 295 rows
per run (291 variant draws plus four harness-control rows), with
`schedule-words.tsv` dumped from the run itself. Pin runs used the unpinned
manifests; verify runs used the pinned manifests, whose sha256 matches the
checked-in ones; pin and verify agree row for row in all four sets.
`clean-alu-m6-*` are the committed manifests run from a fresh `git worktree` at
commit `8a1bd50` with an empty `git status` before and after and the outputs
outside the checkout; all four match their pinned rows and schedule dumps
exactly. Regenerate with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_m6dist.py --set 222|33|123|321` and pin with
`--results DIR`.

### What is measured, inferred and open

* **Measured**: in all four six-packet distributions, the 20 forward cells,
  16 wrap cells, six own-packet negatives, twenty-four non-predecessor pairs,
  nine conflict cases and six baselines behave exactly as the cyclic rule
  predicts for `0 -> 1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 4 -> 5 -> 0`, with the same words in every
  run. In the same runs the M=4 2 + 2 control still returns `0x00` for the
  latch-0 wrap and `0x60` for latch 3, and the M=5 wrap still returns `0x70`.
* **Inferred**: uniform two-packet occupancy is not sufficient to reproduce
  the M=4 2 + 2 shortfall - `2 + 2 + 2` has it and is complete. Neither is a
  two-batch group (`33` is complete), a batch of three packets (`33` and the
  three-packet batch of `123` are complete), a two-packet batch
  (`123` contains one and is complete), batch order (`123`/`321` agree) or the
  wrap endpoints' batch positions (all four shapes differ there and all are
  complete). Among every arrangement measured so far - all seven four-packet
  shapes, all eight five-packet shapes and these four six-packet shapes - the
  shortfall appears only in the four-packet size-2 2 + 2 shape.
* **Open**: the hardware mechanism, which none of these runs observes. The
  measurements are consistent with a property specific to four packets in two
  batches of two, but they do not identify it, and they do not establish the
  behaviour of untested group sizes or distributions (for example
  `1 + 1 + 1 + 2` at size 4, six-packet groups in fp20 rather than fx10, or
  other writer-slot/latch cross-combinations, none of which was measured here).

## 46. Five packets over four batches: the pair's position does not change the cycle

Section 44 measured the eight five-packet splits of two- and three-batch groups
and left the size-4 permutations open. A group of four batches holding five
packets has exactly one two-packet batch, because every batch needs at least
one packet and a batch holds at most three. The four splits are therefore
2 + 1 + 1 + 1, 1 + 2 + 1 + 1, 1 + 1 + 2 + 1 and 1 + 1 + 1 + 2. They keep the
packet count, the batch count, the flush count and the number of single-packet
batches fixed and move the pair from the group's first batch to its last;
2 + 1 + 1 + 1 also puts packet 0 in the pair's batch, and 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 is the
only one in which packet 4 shares the last batch with packet 3.

Hypotheses:

| hypothesis | prediction |
|:--|:--|
| four-batch groups all carry the complete cycle | all four complete |
| the cycle depends on the pair's position | at least one placement restricted |
| the cycle depends on packet 4 sharing its batch with packet 3 | only 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 restricted |

### Shape, manifests, words

`asm/alu_m5_2111.in` ... `asm/alu_m5_1112.in` are whole two-group programs at
`alu_buffer_size` 4. Group A is batches 0 to 3, group B is batches 4 to 7, and
group B's first batch (4) stores group A's rows through the DW. Group B repeats
the same distribution, and the program uses eight batches and ten packet
addresses with no padding.

| set | split | group A batches | pair batch |
|:--|:--|:--|--:|
| 2111 | 2 + 1 + 1 + 1 | packets 0,1 \| 2 \| 3 \| 4 | 0 |
| 1211 | 1 + 2 + 1 + 1 | packet 0 \| 1,2 \| 3 \| 4 | 1 |
| 1121 | 1 + 1 + 2 + 1 | packet 0 \| 1 \| 2,3 \| 4 | 2 |
| 1112 | 1 + 1 + 1 + 2 | packet 0 \| 1 \| 2 \| 3,4 | 3 |

From each run's raw dump (`schedule-words.tsv`: `count@address` per batch):

| set | batch words |
|:--|:--|
| 2111 | `2@0`, `1@2`, `1@3`, `1@4`, `2@5`, `1@7`, `1@8`, `1@9` |
| 1211 | `1@0`, `2@1`, `1@3`, `1@4`, `1@5`, `2@6`, `1@8`, `1@9` |
| 1121 | `1@0`, `1@1`, `2@2`, `1@4`, `1@5`, `1@6`, `2@7`, `1@9` |
| 1112 | `1@0`, `1@1`, `1@2`, `2@3`, `1@5`, `1@6`, `1@7`, `2@8` |

Every count and address matches the intended split in both groups, the ten
packet addresses 0..9 appear once each, and no `0x806` word is non-zero in any
run. The assembler derives all of this from the block placement; no schedule
word is patched, and the numbers above are read back from the hardware runs.

### Matrix coverage

Each manifest (`asm_characterize.alu_m5_2111.json` ... `..._1112.json`, built
by `make_alu_m5dist.py --set 2111|1211|1121|1112`) has 80 variants: 70 matrix
variants and the same ten cross-shape controls. The matrix is the one section
44 uses - 16 forward cells (four edges x four latches), two forward cells
re-read from another slot, 16 wrap cells (packet 4 slot N writes, packet 0
slot S reads aluN, four latches x four reader slots), five own-packet reads,
all 15 non-predecessor source/reader pairs, seven multi-writer cases,
write-only/read-without-writer/unmarked/no-write baselines and five
`row-direct` payload controls - so the four splits differ only in their batch
placement.

As in notes 44 and 45, the wrap couples producer slot N to latch `aluN` and
varies only the reader's slot. Arbitrary producer-slot/latch cross-combinations
are not tested.

Three repeats per variant, and the runner adds the two immediate-operand and
two varying-pipeline harness rows, so each run directory carries 244 result
rows.

### Forward edges and wrap

All 16 forward cells per set returned the source packet's byte for every
latch: 0 -> 1 `0x10`, 1 -> 2 `0x20`, 2 -> 3 `0x40`, 3 -> 4 `0x60`. The
re-reads from another slot returned the same values (`fwd01-al0-s2` `0x10`,
`fwd34-al3-s2` `0x60`).

All 16 wrap cells per set returned `0x70`: packet 4's result reached packet 0
through all four latches, from all four reader slots, in every one of the four
placements. That includes `alu0`, `alu1` and `alu2`, which the M=4 size-2
2 + 2 packing does not carry.

### Non-predecessor reads, conflicts and baselines

All five own-packet reads and all 15 non-predecessor pairs returned `0x00` in
every set. The multi-writer cases returned the immediate predecessor:

| case | writers | measured |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `both-1-2-read3` | packets 1 (`0x20`), 2 (`0x40`) | `0x40` |
| `both-2-3-read4` | packets 2, 3 | `0x60` |
| `both-3-4-read0` | packets 3, 4 | `0x70` |
| `both-2-4-read0` | packets 2, 4 | `0x70` |
| `both-0-4-read2` | packets 0, 4 | `0x00` |
| `three-1-3-4-read0` | packets 1, 3, 4 | `0x70` |
| `three-0-2-3-read4` | packets 0, 2, 3 | `0x60` |

`write-only-p4` returned `0x70`, `read-nowrite`, `unmarked` and `none`
returned `0x00`, and `row-direct-p0` ... `row-direct-p4` returned `0x10`,
`0x20`, `0x40`, `0x60` and `0x70`, validating the five payloads in the same
runs that use them.

### Controls in the same runs

| control | template | measured |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `ctl-rot2` | size 2, one packet per batch | `0x40` |
| `ctl-rot3` | size 3, one packet per batch | `0x7F` |
| `ctl-carry-s1` | size 1 group-boundary carry | `0x10` |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap0` | M=4 2 + 2, latch-0 wrap | `0x00` |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap3` | M=4 2 + 2, latch-3 wrap | `0x60` |
| `ctl-ref31-wrap0` | M=4 3 + 1, latch-0 wrap | `0x60` |
| `ctl-ref31-fwd01` | M=4 3 + 1, packet 0 -> 1 | `0x10` |
| `ctl-ref211-wrap0` | M=4 2 + 1 + 1, latch-0 wrap | `0x60` |
| `ctl-ref5-wrap0` | M=5 2 + 3, packet 4 -> 0 | `0x70` |
| `ctl-ref5-fwd34` | M=5 2 + 3, packet 3 -> 4 | `0x60` |

The M=4 2 + 2 control returned the restricted `0x00` and `0x60` pair in every
one of the four manifests, so the original anomaly was still observable in the
same sessions that measured the four-batch shapes.

### Reproducibility

Pin runs used unpinned manifests at branch head
`35ffc4c06985bc6c6ae6f014722c28339691ecc1`, with `make_alu_m5dist.py`
modified and the new templates and manifests untracked. Each of the four runs
has 244 rows and 244 passes (22:15:23-22:16:43 UTC). Pinning wrote the
manifests with the measured expected words:
`asm_characterize.alu_m5_2111.json` (sha256 `6c5d35e8a11d`),
`..._1211.json` (`5097a2633c04`), `..._1121.json` (`470f992740b7`) and
`..._1112.json` (`4a56477e557f`).

Verify runs used the pinned manifests in the same checkout (22:18:34-22:20:39
UTC) and passed 244/244 in every set; each run's metadata records the manifest
sha256 used.

The assets were committed as `a271664a9cf8b80b13df1789b09577be1099dbc1`, and
clean reproductions ran in a fresh worktree at that commit
(`~/clean-a271664`, empty `git status` before and after) into
`~/cruns/clean-alu-m5-*` (22:22:50-22:25:03 UTC). They passed 244/244 in all
four sets. Pin, verify and clean agree row-for-row, and their schedule dumps
are byte-identical within each set.

### What the measurements say

All four four-batch distributions have the complete five-packet cycle. Moving
the single two-packet batch from the first batch to the last, and changing
whether the wrap endpoints share that batch (2 + 1 + 1 + 1) or whether packet
4 shares the last batch with packet 3 (1 + 1 + 1 + 2), did not restrict the
wrap in any set. The M=5 rotation is therefore complete in all twelve tested
distributions - the eight two- and three-batch splits of section 44 and these
four four-batch splits. Note 47 later showed that the M=4 size-2 2 + 2
restriction was a slot-addressing artifact and that the 2 + 2 shape is
complete too, so no tested four-, five- or six-packet arrangement is
restricted; the comparison with the 2 + 2 control stands, but its "exception"
is withdrawn.

These runs do not identify a hardware mechanism, and they do not establish
that every untested five-packet arrangement or group size behaves the same
way. They also do not test fp20 register formats or the same shapes on another
SoC, and the producer-slot/latch coupling above limits the wrap matrix to one
writer slot per latch.

## 47. The size-2 2 + 2 wrap, with writer slot, read latch and reader slot separated

Section 38 reported that a four-packet group in two two-packet batches wrapped
from packet 3 to packet 0 only through `alu3`, and sections 39, 43, 44, 45 and
46 treated that as the one restricted M = 4 packing. The `wrap`, `probe` and
`probe2` sets did vary the latch and the reading slot, but every variant that
was meant to write "packet 3 slot 0, 1 or 2" wrote `B0`, `B1` or `B2`. Batch 1
holds packets 2 and 3, so `B0..B3` are packet 2's slots; those cells were
two-packets-apart reads, not wraps. Only `B7` (packet 3 slot 3) addressed the
group's last packet. This set separates the three dimensions that can vary -
packet 3's writing slot P, the latch L that packet 0 reads, and packet 0's
reading slot R - over all 64 combinations.

The ISA does not allow an arbitrary write latch. The issuing slot selects the
latch and the destination field does not route: a write issued from slot 1 is
readable as `alu1` whichever destination code 24..27 names, and not as `alu0`
(section 17). The matrix therefore varies P, L and R, but "latch written"
cannot vary independently of P because that combination cannot be encoded.
Where L = P the cell is an ordinary wrap cell. Where L != P packet 3 never
wrote `aluL`, so a non-zero result would mean the wrap re-indexes a value
between latches.

Hypotheses:

| hypothesis | prediction |
|:--|:--|
| success follows the writer slot | every L succeeds when P = 3; all P < 3 fail |
| success follows the read latch | one L succeeds for every P |
| success follows the reader slot | some R fails for fixed P and L |
| success needs P = L | exactly the 16 diagonal cells succeed, from all four R |
| the old restriction was real | only P = 3 with L = 3 succeeds |

### Shape, manifests, words

`asm/alu_pack4.in` is unchanged: six batches at `alu_buffer_size` 2, group A in
batches 0-1, group B in batches 2-3, group C in batches 4-5. Batch 0 holds
packets 0 and 1; batch 1 holds packets 2 and 3. Packet 3's slot P is `B{4+P}`,
packet 0's slot R is `A{R}`, and group B's first batch stores group A's row
through `store rt1, r0, r1`. Every cell writes `0x60` (u3) in exactly one slot
and reads exactly one latch from exactly one slot; every other ALU slot is
`NOP`. No schedule word is patched.

Every one of the 71 variants uploads the same six batch words in every repeat
(the `ref31` and `ref211` controls use their own templates):

| batch | word | meaning |
|--:|--:|:--|
| 0 | `0x00000002` | count 2, packet address 0 |
| 1 | `0x0000000A` | count 2, packet address 2 |
| 2 | `0x00000011` | count 1, packet address 4 |
| 3 | `0x00000015` | count 1, packet address 5 |
| 4 | `0x00000019` | count 1, packet address 6 |
| 5 | `0x0000001D` | count 1, packet address 7 |

So group A is four packets in two batches of two, group B repeats the shape and
stores group A's rows, and every `0x806` complement word is zero.

### Matrix coverage and controls

`make_alu_pack4.py --set m4matrix` writes
`asm_characterize.alu_pack4_m4matrix.json`: 64 matrix variants named
`m4x-pP-alL-sR` plus seven variant controls, for 71 variants. Three repeats per
variant and the runner's two immediate-operand and two varying-pipeline harness
rows give 217 rows per run, 213 of them variant rows.

| control | expression | result | note |
|:--|:--|--:|:--|
| `ctl-ref22-wrap3` | `B7` writes 0x60, `A3` reads alu3 | `0x60` | the exact known-positive cell from section 38, identical to `m4x-p3-al3-s3` |
| `ctl-ref22-wrap0` | `B0` writes 0x60, `A0` reads alu0 | `0x00` | the exact known-negative from section 38, identical to `m4x-p0-al0-s0`; with the corrected slot table it is a packet 2 -> packet 0 read |
| `ctl-ref22-fwd` | `B0` writes 0x40, `B4` reads alu0 | `0x40` | the known forward edge, packet 2 -> packet 3, unchanged |
| `ctl-ref31-wrap` | `alu_pack43.in`: `B0` writes 0x60, `A0` reads alu0 | `0x60` | the 3 + 1 wrap from section 39, on its own template |
| `ctl-ref211-wrap` | `alu_dist211.in`: `C0` writes 0x60, `A0` reads alu0 | `0x60` | the size-3 2 + 1 + 1 wrap from section 43, on its own template |
| `ctl-row-direct-60` | `B7` writes 0x60, batch 2 stores r2 | `0x60` | direct-row payload control: the write lands and the store path carries 0x60 |
| `none` | no write, batch 2 stores r0 | `0x00` | word baseline |
| harness | immediate-operand / varying-pipeline | `0xFF` / `0xFF01FF00` | before and after every run |

The known positive and negative reproduce their section 38 words exactly, so
the run is valid for the matrix interpretation; what changes is what the
negative means.

### Results: the full 4 x 4 x 4 outcome matrix

Every cell writes packet 3 slot P (filling `aluP`) and reads `aluL` from
packet 0 slot R. Rows are writer slot and read latch; the four columns are the
reader slot.

| P | L | R=0 | R=1 | R=2 | R=3 |
|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| 0 | 0 | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` |
| 0 | 1 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 0 | 2 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 0 | 3 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 1 | 0 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 1 | 1 | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` |
| 1 | 2 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 1 | 3 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 2 | 0 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 2 | 1 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 2 | 2 | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` |
| 2 | 3 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 3 | 0 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 3 | 1 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 3 | 2 | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` | `0x00` |
| 3 | 3 | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` | `0x60` |

Analysis:

* **Producer slot alone does not decide.** P = 0, 1 and 2 succeed for their own
  latch, while P = 3 fails for L = 0, 1 and 2. A "last slot only" rule is
  contradicted by the P < 3 diagonal.
* **Read latch alone does not decide.** L = 3 is 0 for P = 0, 1 and 2, and
  L = 0..2 succeed for their matching P.
* **Reader slot never decides.** Each of the 16 (P, L) pairs returns the same
  word from all four reader slots.
* **The interaction is exactly P = L.** Success is the 16 diagonal cells;
  failure is the remaining 48. There is no three-way interaction, and no
  partial success inside a cell.
* **No cross-latch delivery.** The 48 off-diagonal cells read a latch nothing
  in group A wrote and return 0, so the wrap does not move a value from the
  writer's latch to another index.

Because the write latch is selected by the writer slot, "P = L" is precisely
"read the latch that the group's last packet wrote in that slot". The matrix
therefore shows a complete wrap for every one of group A's four latches, from
every reader slot, and the only restriction is the one the ISA imposes on
writes: a packet's slot N writes `aluN` and nothing else.

### Why the earlier reading was wrong

`asm/alu_pack4.in` names batch 1's two packets `B0..B3` and `B4..B7`, and the
schedule words above (`2@0` then `2@2`) place packets 2 and 3 there. The
section 38 sets already contain the control that exposes the mislabelling:
`pair-p2-p0` writes `B0` with 0x40 and reads `A0`, and returns 0 as a
two-packets-apart negative, exactly as `src3-read0` (B0 with 0x60, `A0`) does.
`both-own-prev`, also a `B0` write, returns 0. The one cell that wrote the
group's last packet, `alu3-from-p3-slot3` (`B7`), is the one that returned
0x60.

So the section 38 conclusion was an addressing error, not a hardware
restriction: the wrap probes covered packet 3 slot 3 and packet 2 slots 0-2,
and the resulting 0s were read as missing latches. That error propagated into
sections 39-46, which used the 2 + 2 negative as a control and explained why
other packings did not reproduce it. The measurements in those sections are
unaffected - every one of them re-measured a `B0` write and got 0 - but the
interpretation "only latch 3 wraps in 2 + 2" is withdrawn.

### Reproducibility

Pinned from actual hardware on 2026-11-23 (Toshiba AC100 / Dynabook AZ, Linux
`kusma-ac100 7.2.0-gd1efa725955b`, armv7l):

* pin run: unmodified `asm/alu_pack4.in`, generator modified, new manifest
  untracked, commit `cbb718c`; 213/213 variant rows passed.
* verify run: the pinned manifest (`sha256 02f35108deeb...`), same commit;
  213/213 passed, manifest hash matches the run metadata.
* clean run: fresh worktree at the asset commit `700a4a7`, `git status` empty
  before and after, 213/213 passed.

Pin, verify and clean agree row for row and iteration for iteration (217 rows
each), and the three `schedule-words.tsv` files are byte-identical. Control
rows passed before and after every run.

Artifacts: `asm_characterize.alu_pack4_m4matrix.json`,
`results/pin-alu-pack4-m4matrix/`, `results/verify-alu-pack4-m4matrix/`,
`results/clean-alu-pack4-m4matrix/`, each with `metadata.json`, `results.tsv`
and `schedule-words.tsv`. The per-row stdout and stderr files are in each run
directory's `logs/`, which `results/.gitignore` excludes (`*/logs/` and
`*/generated/`), as for every other run: the committed directories carry the
metadata, results and schedule dumps only, and the `logs/...` paths in
`results.tsv` are relative to the run directory. Regenerate with
`python3 tests/grate/asm/make_alu_pack4.py --set m4matrix` and pin with
`--results DIR`.

### What this settles

* **Measured:** the M = 4 size-2 2 + 2 wrap is complete for all four latches
  and all four reader slots. Success requires reading the latch that packet 3
  writes in its own slot; the wrap never re-indexes a value.
* **Measured:** the reader slot is irrelevant to the wrap at this packing.
* **Measured:** the section 38 positive (`B7` -> alu3) and negative (`B0` ->
  alu0) still return 0x60 and 0.
* **Deduced from the template and the schedule words:** the earlier "only
  latch 3" result came from probing packet 2's slots while labelling them
  packet 3's. The 3 + 1 and 2 + 1 + 1 wraps in the same manifest confirm the
  harness reaches the store.
* **Withdrawn:** "size-2 2 + 2 is the only restricted M = 4 packing". Every
  tested M = 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 packing now has the full rotation.

Remaining uncertainty: the writer slot/latch coupling means the "latch" axis
cannot be varied independently, so these data cannot say whether a write path
that selected an arbitrary latch would wrap. The mechanism of the rotation
itself is unchanged and unmeasured; fp20, T114 and untested packet distributions
remain open, as does the group-boundary carry. The scheduling consequence is
conservative: a wrap edge is available for the group's last packet's latch, at
that same slot index and from any reader slot, in every tested packing; any
other edge requires a row.

## 48. ALU bit 56: `(sat)` clamps the result to 0..1

**Question.** The ALU encoding names bit 56 "saturate result" and the assembler
accepts `(sat)`, but the behaviour had never been measured. A backend needs to
know whether the bit clamps, to what range, in which destination formats, and
where in the result path it sits relative to the result scale and the condition
code. This matters because a shader that has to clamp a value can otherwise only
spell the clamp as a `MIN`/`MAX` pair.

**Probe.** `asm_characterize.alu_saturate.json`, template
`asm/alu_saturate.in`, 16 variants, three repeats each, 52 rows including the
runner's before/after controls. Three batches at `alu_buffer_size = 1`: batch 0
prefills `r2` with 0.5, batch 1 runs the tested instruction into `r2.lh` or
`r2.l*`, and batch 2 reads `r2` and normalises it into `r3` before
`store rt1, r2, r3`. The normalising batch is what makes values above 1.0
visible: the readback shows the fp20 code of `r2` in byte 1 and of `r3` in
byte 3 (`0xF7` = 1.0, `0xFF` = 2.0, `0xFB` = 1.5, `0xF3` = 0.75, `0xEF` = 0.5,
`0xE7` = 0.25), so a clamped 2.0 reads `0xF7` instead of `0xFF` even though the
8-bit colour conversion would have saturated both.

| variant | tested operation | without `(sat)` | with `(sat)` |
|:--|:--|--:|--:|
| above range | `MAD r2.lh, u0(2.0), #1, #0, #1` | `0xEF00FF00` | `0xE700F700` |
| below range | `MAD r2.lh, u4(-0.5), #1, #0, #1`, norm `-r2 * 0.5` | `0xE7000000` | `0x00000000` |
| inside range | `MAD r2.lh, u3(0.5), #1, #0, #1` | not run (same value) | `0xE700EF00` |
| result scale | `MAD r2.lh, u2(0.75), #1, #0, #1 (x2)` | `0xF300FB00` | `0xEF00F700` |
| condition | `MAD r2.lh, u4(-0.5), #1, #0, #1 (ge)` | `0x00000000` | `0xF700F700` |
| condition (eq) | `MAD r2.lh, u0(2.0), #1, #0, #1 (eq)` | `0x00000000` | `0x00000000` |
| MAX | `MAX r2.lh, u0(2.0), #1, #0, #1` | not run (2.0, `0xFF00FF00`) | `0xF700F700` |
| MUL | `MUL r2.lh, u2(0.75), u0(2.0), #0, #1` | `0xF300FB00` | `0xEF00F700` |
| fx10 destination | `MAD r2.l*, u0(2.0), #1, #0, #1`, norm `r2.l * 0.25` | `0xEE0000FF` | `0xE70000FF` |

Reading it:

* **it clamps to exactly 0.0 and 1.0.** 2.0 becomes 1.0 (`0xFF` -> `0xF7`) and
  the normalised product halves from 0.5 to 0.25. -0.5 becomes 0.0: the
  normalised `-r2 * 0.5` falls from 0.25 (`0xE7`) to zero. A value already
  inside the range is unchanged (0.5 stays `0xEF`).
* **the clamp is after the result scale.** 0.75 with `(x2)` writes 1.5
  (`0xFB`) without the bit and 1.0 (`0xF7`) with it, so the scale is applied
  first and the clamp sees its output. Clamping the operand first would have
  left 0.75 and then stored 1.5.
* **the clamp is before the condition code.** -0.5 with `(ge)` alone is false
  and stores 0.0; with `(ge)(sat)` it stores 1.0, because the condition tests
  the clamped 0.0 (`== 0`, so `>= 0` is true). The condition result is itself
  0.0 or 1.0, so saturating it changes nothing - which is why the `(eq)` row on
  2.0 reads zero either way (1.0 clamps to 1.0 and is not equal to zero).
* **it applies to `MUL`, `MAX` and the fp20 and fx10 write formats, not just
  `MAD`.** The fx10 row needs the normalised `r3` to show it: the raw low byte
  of `r2` reads `0xFF` for both 1.0 and 2.0 because the 8-bit conversion of an
  fx10 value saturates either way, while `r3` is 0.25 with the bit and 0.5
  without. `MIN` was not run in that experiment; note 49 measures it separately and
  confirms the clamp applies there too.
* **the negative raw code is not visible in the readback.** `r2` reads zero for
  -0.5 with and without the bit, because the fp20 sign is outside the 10-bit
  field the DW conversion shows. The clamp at zero is established by the
  normalised `r3`, not by `r2`.

**Order of the result path.** Operation, result scale, saturate, condition,
write. The four stages are separated by the rows above; only the position of
saturate relative to the condition code reverses an observable value.

**What this does not establish.** The accumulate path (`(this)`/`(other)`) was
not combined with `(sat)`, a condition-register destination was not tested, and
values outside the fp20 range above 1.0 or below 0.0 were not run beyond the
two endpoints (2.0 and -0.5). The clamp endpoint is exactly 1.0 and 0.0 in the
tested range, and no saturation-of-saturation or `MIN` case was measured.

**Reproducibility.** Pin and verify runs at manifest sha256
`7e106294804a36be459dd7ddc60d85596392df22019bb65008ac0143756823f3` agree on
all 52 rows; the controls (`immediate-operand` `0x000000FF`,
`varying-pipeline` `0xFF01FF00`) pass before and after. The committed run
directories are `results/pin-alu-saturate`, `results/verify-alu-saturate` and
`results/clean-alu-saturate`; the per-row logs are untracked, as for every
other run (`results/.gitignore`).

## 49. ALU MIN and MAX operands

The opcode table describes `MIN` as `min(rA*rB, rC*rD)` and `MAX` as the
maximum of the same two products, but that wording came from the encoder's
field layout, never from a measurement. The operation matters directly for
GLSL ES `min()` and `max()`, so it was run with the three-batch ALU harness:
batch 0 prefills `r2` with 0.5, batch 1 runs the operation into `r2.lh`, batch 2
copies the result into `r3` and `store rt1, r2, r3` reads both back. The stored
word puts each register's fp20 code in the high byte of its 16-bit half: r2's
code in byte 1 and r3's in byte 3, as note 48 describes. The fp20 codes are
`0xE7` 0.25, `0xEF` 0.5, `0xF3` 0.75, `0xF7` 1.0; the asymmetric negative rows
below read `0xF3000000`, i.e. r3 = 0.75 in byte 3 and r2 = 0 because the sign
is outside the bits the DW conversion shows.

The template is `tests/grate/asm/alu_minmax.in` and the manifest is
`tests/grate/asm_characterize.alu_minmax.json`
(sha256 `46739e8e6ff9da63b322657376ab9d2a70c152920235b608955259bbc07e598e`).
Constants are exact in fp20: `u0` 0.5, `u1` 0.25, `u2` 0.75, `u3` 1.0,
`u4` 2.0.

**Both products are multiplied.** `MIN r2.lh, u0, u0, u2, #1` compares
0.5\*0.5 = 0.25 with 0.75 and stores 0.25. A plain operand minimum would be
min(0.5, 0.75) = 0.5. `MAX r2.lh, u0, u4, u2, #1` compares 1.0 with 0.75 and
stores 1.0; a plain operand maximum would store 0.75. Both `rB` and `rC` are
multipliers, not spare fields:

| variant | operation | value |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `min-products` | min(0.5\*0.5, 0.75\*1) | 0.25 (`0xE7`) |
| `max-products` | max(0.5\*2.0, 0.75\*1) | 1.0 (`0xF7`) |
| `min-rB` | min(0.5\*2.0, 0.75\*1) | 0.75 (`0xF3`) |
| `min-rB-product` | min(0.25\*2.0, 0.75\*1) | 0.5 (`0xEF`) |
| `min-rC-dominates` | min(2.0\*0.5, 0.25\*1) | 0.25 (`0xE7`) |
| `min-plain` | min(0.25\*1, 0.5\*1) | 0.25 (`0xE7`) |
| `max-plain` | max(0.25\*1, 0.5\*1) | 0.5 (`0xEF`) |

`min-rC-dominates` is the row that proves the second term is read at all: the
first product is 1.0, so the 0.25 can only come from `rC`.

**The fourth source is a one-bit constant, not a register.** The encoder's
`rD_reg_select` field is one bit wide (`fragment_asm.h`), and the assembler
accepts only `#1` in the fourth slot; `#0` fails with "Invalid src register".
The committed template therefore spells the practical form
`MIN dst, rA, rB, rC, #1`, which computes `min(rA*rB, rC)`. Hand-built words
that moved the opcode or `rD` bits did not reproduce the symbolic instruction
and are not reported as evidence; what the hardware does with `rD != 1` remains
unmeasured.

**Negative results and result scale.**
`MIN r2.lh, -u2, u3, u0, #1` and `MAX r2.lh, -u2, u3, -u0, #1` both select a
negative value. As in note 48 the raw `r2` code reads zero because the sign is
outside the bits the readback shows, while the normalised `r3` (negate the
result into batch 2) shows the magnitude: 0.75 and 0.5 respectively. Both
operations therefore work on signed fp20 values. `MIN (x2)` on 0.25 stores
0.5, so the result scale is applied after the selection.

**Saturation applies to MIN.** `MIN r2.lh, -u2, u3, u0, #1 (sat)` stores zero,
and the follow-up `r2 + 1` reads 1.0. This closes the "MIN shares the clamp path
by inference" caveat in note 48: on this path the clamp is measured.

**What this does not establish.** `rD` other than `#1`, `CSEL`, NaN/Inf
operands, an fx10 destination, condition codes on `MIN`/`MAX`, and the
accumulate path were not run. The absolute-value syntax was not accepted in the
form tried, so source modifiers other than negate are untested here.

**Reproducibility.** Pin and verify runs at the manifest sha256 above agree on
all 34 rows (11 variants x 3 repeats plus four controls), and the clean run
from a worktree checked out at the commit containing the manifest
(`2e509e51b7a45f20b96569914de7929148f09363`) gives the same words with an empty
`git_status`. Committed run directories are `results/pin-minmax`,
`results/verify-minmax` and `results/clean-minmax`; the per-row logs are
untracked as for every other run.

## What the runner cost

Five things that produced wrong results first:

* **an unencodable instruction looks like a hardware result.** The first
  `MUL` sweep used four uniforms and every variant exited 1 with
  `fs: line N: Invalid src register`; the runner records that as `exit-error`,
  not as a result, which is the only reason it was not mistaken for a colour.
  Check the status of the first repeat before reading any word.
* **`expected` has to be pinned from a measured run, not from a guess.** Two
  words were written the wrong way round in this session (`0x007F0040` for
  `0x7F000040`) because the DW byte order was applied from the wrong end. Run
  once with `expected` omitted, read `results.tsv`, then pin: the second run is
  the proof, the first is only a measurement. The reverse also bites - a word
  pinned from a run whose *environment* was wrong is worse than no pin, because
  it turns a generator bug into a failing manifest.
* **a shadowed variable in a generator is a hardware mystery.** Adding a
  `--count` override to `make_alu_buffer_matrix.py` reused the loop variable's
  name, so every variant after the first was poked with the first variant's
  count (4, the size-1 count). Sizes 3 and 4 then returned zero from every store
  for a whole run, and at size 3 they hung, and the first conclusion written
  down was "sizes 3 and 4 are not reproducible". They were: the manifest was.
  The fix is one variable name; the lesson is that when one run disagrees with
  another, check what the manifest *actually asked for* (here:
  `python3 -c 'import json,collections; ...'` over `GRATE_POKE`) before
  concluding that the hardware changed its mind.
* **one hang invalidates the rest of the run, not just itself.** A count that
  leaves an incomplete final group (4 batches at size 3) timed out, and every later variant
  in that run timed out too - including variants that pass on their own in a
  fresh run - because the wedged channel does not recover while the manifest is
  still going. The runner reports those as ordinary failures. Keep known-risky
  variants last, or in their own manifest. After a *killed* run the channel
  needs the kernel's 10 s CDMA timeout per wedged job before anything works
  again, and until then every draw fails with `****nvrm_init failed****` from
  the assembler, which looks like a broken device rather than a wedged one.
* **a uniform-driven probe needs the immediate-operand control in the same
  run.** `fs_alu_immediate_control.txt` is that control (it must read
  `0x000000FF`) and it is a control in all three manifests, next to the shipped
  varying case (`0xFF01FF00`), because a board that has lost the constant path
  returns a plausible `f(0)` instead of an error.

## 50. `gl_FragCoord.z`: the `(z)` link does not populate TRAM

The two documented halves of the z path are the linker's "magic write" of
VEC4.z into TRAM0.w and the fragment-side reconstruction
`1/1000 + max(0,r3.lo)/4000 + max(0,r3.hi)/4`. Note 10 could not see the write
because every `(z)` test used position z = 0. This note repeats the test with a
controlled clip-space z and a readback that is calibrated against known exports.

The probe writes position as `(attribute.xy, c[100].z, 1)` in the vertex
shader, with the z overwrite in its own EXEC because the assembler accepts only
one masked vector write per EXEC. The fragment primes r0..r3, fetches one TRAM
component with `ipl`, and stores r2/r3 unconverted. `tram0.w` is read for the
z sweep, and x/y/z/w are each read once at z = 0.75. `GRATE_POKE` sets the
`0x520` batch count as in the TRAM manifest, and the two standard controls run
in the same manifest.

Calibration through the ordinary (no `(z)`) link fixes the word layout and the
fp20 codes: the stored word is `r3 << 16 | r2`, the fetched w lands in r3, and

| value written to tram0.w | word | r3 |
|:--|--:|--:|
| 0.875 (`vs_tram.txt`, export1) | `0xF500E700` | `0xF500` |
| 0.25 (`vs_tram_b.txt`, export1) | `0xE700E700` | `0xE700` |
| position w = 1.0 (no `(z)`, z = 0.75) | `0xF700E700` | `0xF700` |
| prime r3 = 0.375 | `0xEB00` (from `z-x-z075`) | `0xEB00` |

With `(z)` and all four columns fp20, the components do **not** carry position
z. At z = 0.75 the fetched x is `0x0000` and y, z and w are all `0x2000`; at
other z values the w column reads:

| clip z | 0.25 | 0.5 | 0.625 | 0.7 | 0.75 | 0.8 | 0.875 | 0.99 | 1.0 | -0.5 | -1.0 |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| tram0.w | `1800` | `0000` | `1800` | `1C00` | `2000` | `0000` | `0000` | `0000` | `0000` | `0000` | `0000` |

The three repeats of each row are identical, so this is not sampling noise, but
the sequence is not monotonic in z and does not match any calibrated fp20 code:
window z 0.875 would be `0xF500`, not `0x0000` or `0x2000`. Whatever the
`0x1800`/`0x1C00`/`0x2000` family is, it is not position z expressed in the
format the TRAM fetch decodes.

The all-NOP recipe from the extended record is decisive. With
`LINK NOP, NOP, NOP, NOP, tram0.xyzw, export0 (z)` and `sat(NOP)` in the w slot,
r3 reads `0x0000` for z = 0, 0.5, 0.75 and 1.0; without `(z)` it also reads 0.
Reading tram0.w in the same all-NOP configuration returns `0xF700`, i.e. 1.0,
the position's w. So `(z)` leaves the link writing position-derived data that
is not z, and the dedicated `sat(NOP)` fetch does not receive a depth value
either.

Conclusion: the linker `(z)` modifier does not deliver position z through TRAM,
and the §6.6 fragment reconstruction has no measured input on this path. That
matches the parenthetical in `docs/shader-linking.md` ("actual TRAM seems
untouched"). Earlier z = 0 probes reading zero are explained by the position
value, but a non-zero z does not make the value usable. Whether the rasterizer
supplies `gl_FragCoord.z` through some other register or side channel is still
**Unknown**; no tested link produced a z-derived TRAM word.

Two earlier attempts are discarded rather than fitted: one exported position
before the z overwrite, and one packed a whole register pair through the
fp20/fx10 conversion and could not be decoded byte-for-byte. Both are excluded
from the tables above. The retained runs are the observe directories
`results/observe3-fragz` (31 rows) and `results/observe4-fragz` (61 rows) plus
`results/observe5-fragz` (20 rows); the first two 31-row attempts failed on a
vertex-template parse error and are not evidence.

Follow-up: note 52 ran the same probes with the depth pipeline enabled, which
shows the rasterizer receives the clip z correctly, and found the actual fragment
path in the ordinary link copy of the position export. The `0x1800`/`0x1C00`/
`0x2000` family is the `(z)` magic write, not a z encoding.

## 51. Cube-map sampling: the harness has no cube binding path

Cube maps are required by GLES2 and are still uncharacterized. The limitation
is in the test driver, not an observation about AR20:

* `tools/assembler.c` binds one 2D surface with `--texture`. The only
  constructor is `grate_create_texture(grate, width, height, format, layout)`,
  and `struct grate_texture` carries a width, height and pitch. There is no
  six-face allocation and no cube flag.
* The texture descriptor words are `TEXTURE_DESC1` at `0x720 + 2i` and
  `TEXTURE_DESC2` at `0x721 + 2i`. The field set in `include/tgr_3d.xml.h` is
  format, filters, wraps, mipmap disable, max LOD, width/height (log2) and
  NOT_POW2_DIMENSIONS. There is no dimension, cube or face-enable field, and no
  cube face pointer array.
* `GRATE_TEX_FORMAT` accepts `RGBA8888`, `dxt1`, `etc1` and `d16`, all linear
  2D surfaces; `GRATE_TEX_SIZE` is `WxH`. No assembler option selects a cube
  target.
* `src/libgrate` contains no cube texture code at all.

`GRATE_POKE` can write arbitrary registers after texture setup, but the bit
that would select cube behaviour is exactly what is unknown. Setting a guessed
bit without six bound faces would sample the 2D surface and could not
distinguish "AR20 ignores cube maps" from "the guess was wrong"; that is not a
probe that separates the competing interpretations.

The smallest credible test-only addition is therefore not small: allocate six
face surfaces with distinct contents, teach the descriptor builder a cube
dimension, and bind the face pointers. The descriptor field for that dimension
is not documented here, so the change would itself be an ISA investigation, not
a harness tweak. Until it exists, cube-face selection, direction-vector use and
the descriptor contract remain **Unknown**, and the 2D R-coordinate and
inert-bit results stay scoped to the four linear formats they were measured
with.

## 52. `gl_FragCoord.z`: the position export's z lane is the fragment z

Note 50 tested the `(z)` link select and found no z in TRAM, but every one of
those runs had the depth pipeline disabled. The assembler can bind a D16 depth
surface and enable depth test and write (`GRATE_DEPTH=1`), and it prints the
depth buffer back (`depth16`). That is an independent witness for the value the
rasterizer computed, and it changes the picture: the rasterizer receives the
vertex position and computes the window z correctly, so the clip z is present
and well defined, while the fragment side still has to be found.

Setup on the AC100 (Tegra 20 / AR20, `kusma-ac100 7.2.0-gd1efa725955b`): the
vertex template writes position with a controlled clip z, the fragment primes
r0..r3, links gl_Position (`export0`) into `tram0.xyzw` with one fp20 per
column, fetches one lane and stores r2/r3 raw. The same manifests run with and
without `GRATE_DEPTH`. The standard controls (`immediate-operand`,
`varying-pipeline`) pass before and after each sweep.

Depth witness, clip z -> `depth16` (viewport scale 0.5, bias 0.5, so the
window value is z/2 + 0.5):

| clip z | 0.25 | 0.5 | 0.75 | 0.875 | 1.0 |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| depth16 | `9FFF` | `BFFF` | `DFFF` | `EFFF` | `FFFF` |
| window z | 0.625 | 0.75 | 0.875 | 0.9375 | 1.0 |

The depth value moves exactly as the controlled clip z does, so the probe is
not writing into a dead position path and the z the driver would need exists.

The plain link copy carries it. With `LINK fp20, fp20, fp20, fp20,
tram0.xyzw, export0` (no `(z)`) and the z lane fetched into r2, the stored high
byte is:

| clip z | 0 | 0.125 | 0.25 | 0.375 | 0.5 | 0.625 | 0.75 | 0.875 | 1.0 | -0.5 |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| z lane | `00` | `DF` | `E7` | `EB` | `EF` | `F1` | `F3` | `F5` | `F7` | `00` |

The w lane of the same row reads `F7` (1.0) at z = 0.75, and the x and y lanes
read 0. The code is the calibrated one: the high byte equals `(fp20 >> 10) - 1`
at every tested point (fp20(0.25) = 0x3A000 -> 0xE7, fp20(0.5) = 0x3C000 ->
0xEF, fp20(1.0) = 0x3E000 -> 0xF7), and the low byte is 0 or `FF` depending on
the pixel. The store path therefore keeps only the top bits of the fp20 lane:
the readback resolves a few percent, not the full value, and a negative z is
indistinguishable from zero (the -0.5 row stores 0). Within that resolution the
lane is monotonic in clip z, and the same sweep run with the depth buffer off
gives the same codes, so the lane is not a by-product of depth testing.

That the lane is really the interpolated position z, not one vertex's value or
a uniform echo, is shown by a sloped triangle. The vertex shader writes
`z = 0.5 + x/4`, so clip z runs 0.25 at the left edge to 0.75 at the right.
The fragment stores the z lane to linear `rt4` and `GRATE_REGION` dumps the
rectangle, giving at x = 0, 15 and 30:

| pixel | 0 | 15 | 30 |
|:--|--:|--:|--:|
| z lane | `E7` | `EE` | `F2` |

The lane follows the slope across the quad. The depth buffer in the same run
also varies, so the rasterizer and the fragment see the same interpolation.

Perspective is separate from the lane. With clip z = 0.5 and a uniform w = 2
(one `ADDv` of (0, 0, 0.5, 1); the quad shrinks to NDC +-0.5, so the centre
pixel is read through `rt4`), the z lane reads `EF` (0.5, the clip z, not 0.25)
and the w lane reads `FF` (2.0), while `depth16` reads `9FFF`, i.e. window
z = 0.625 = (0.5/2)*0.5 + 0.5. So the lanes are clip-space z and w, and window
z is the shader's `(z/w)*scale + bias` with the viewport scale and bias the
driver already supplies. The MFU's rcp gives the reciprocal for the divide.

The `(z)` select is not this path and should not be used. With the documented
one-lane form, `LINK NOP, NOP, NOP, fp20, tram0.xyzw, export0 (z)`, reading the
w lane returns `0x2000E700`: the w lane is a near-zero code (0x2000), whereas
the same link without `(z)` returns `0xF700E700` (w = 1.0). Enabling the x lane
instead of w does not help; w still reads 0x2000. So the select does trigger a
write to TRAM0.w, as the linker description says, but the value written is not
the position z on this hardware, and it replaces the normal copy. That also
explains the `0x1800`/`0x1C00`/`0x2000` family in note 50: it is the `(z)`
magic write, not a z encoding. Note 50's all-NOP `sat(NOP)` result is unchanged
by the depth state: r3 reads 0 for z = 0, 0.5, 0.75 and 1.0 with depth test and
write enabled, so the old r3 recipe has no input on this path.

Contract for a driver, measured end to end:

1. Vertex: write gl_Position normally (export 0).
2. Linker: copy the position export into a TRAM row, one fp20 per column, with
   no `(z)` select.
3. Fragment: fetch the row's z and w lanes with `ipl` (`VAR1` fp20); the z lane
   is the interpolated clip z and the w lane the interpolated clip w.
4. Window z: divide z by w and apply the viewport scale and bias. The depth
   buffer confirms the result (0.5/2 -> 0x9FFF with scale and bias 0.5).

Not established: whether the interpolation is perspective-correct for a
varying w (the w = 2 probe is uniform, and the vertex unit accepts only one
masked write per export, so a per-vertex w probe needs a different vertex
template); the exact fp20 code of the lane beyond the coarse readback; and
whether a fragment can obtain window z without the divide (the depth buffer
suggests the hardware computes it, but no register or fetch exposes it).

Discarded runs, not evidence: two attempts to vary w per vertex failed to
parse because the vertex assembler allows only one masked write per export and
the `MADv`/`MULv` masked forms are rejected; those failed runs are not retained.
One `MOVv r63.**zw` attempt parsed but broadcast w into both lanes (the depth
buffer read 0xFFFF, i.e. z = w). The retained runs are `observe6-fragz-depth` (depth sweep, 25 runs), `observe7-fragz-pos`
(plain-copy lanes, 26 runs), `observe8-fragz-zpath` (z sweep without depth, 35
runs), `observe9-fragz-slope` (slope, rt4 region, 11 runs),
`observe12-fragz-w` (the discarded `**zw` probe, kept as the negative result),
`observe13-fragz-copy` (baseline z/w of the harness quad), `observe14-fragz-w`
(ADDv z = 0.5 / w = 2, 14 runs) and `observe15-fragz-zone` (one-lane `(z)`,
12 runs).

## 53. `sfu: rcp r10`: the index is not a row, and the cube-coordinate word does not compute its disassembly

The proprietary compiler emits `0x28689402 0x00000000` for a uniform `vec3`
samplerCube coordinate. Decoded with `fragment_asm.h` it is one MFU word:
`sfu: rcp r10`, `mul0: r0, r2, r0`, `mul1: r1, r2, r1`. Read as ordinary rows
that means "r10 = 1/coordinate, then scale r0 and r1 by r10". This note measures
the word on hardware and finds that reading unsupported: the fragment unit does
not read the row the word names, and the word's two multipliers do not compute
their operands at all while that sfu sub-instruction is in the word.

Measured on the Toshiba AC100 (`7.2.0-gd1efa725955b`, Tegra 20, AR20 fragment
path) with `~/build/grate-exp`, three repeats per row, controls before and after
every manifest (`immediate-operand` `0x000000FF`, `varying-pipeline`
`0xFF01FF00`). All runs below report `success: true`; the raw words, generated
`.asm`, stdout and stderr are under `characterization/` as named.

### 53.1 The store readback, calibrated

`store rt1, r0, r1` writes `code(r0)` in byte 1 and `code(r1)` in byte 3 with
the residue byte 0. `tests/grate/asm/calib_store.in` seeds both rows with the
same constant, so the word reads the same whichever half is which
(`calib-store-20261125T064321Z`):

| value | word | value | word |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.0 | `0x00000000` | 0.5 | `0xEF00EF00` |
| 0.0625 | `0xD700D700` | 0.75 | `0xF300F300` |
| 0.125 | `0xDF00DF00` | 1.0 | `0xF700F700` |
| 0.25 | `0xE700E700` | 1.5 | `0xFB00FB00` |
| 0.375 | `0xEB00EB00` | 2.0, 3.0, 4.0 | `0xFF00FF00` |

So `code = 0xF7 + 8*log2(value)` and anything at or above 2.0 clips to `FF`.
The residue byte is zero for every value an ALU `.lh` write produces; the
`0x10`..`0x60` bytes in the earlier multiplier sweeps came from `.l*` (fx10)
writes, which put the payload in the other half of the row.

### 53.2 What the sfu index reads

`tests/grate/asm/mfu_src_map.in`: batch 0 seeds r0 = 2.0, r1 = 16.0 and r3 =
0.25, batch 1 runs one `sfu: rcp rN` and multiplies it by 1.0 into r2, and the
same batch stores r2 with the r3 marker (`E7`). `mfu-src-map-20261125T063712Z`:

| index | low lane | reading |
|--:|:--|:--|
| 0 | `EF` | r0, 1/2.0 = 0.5 - its own row |
| 1 | `D7` | r1, 1/16.0 = 0.0625 - its own row |
| 3 | `FF` | r3, 1/0.25 = 4.0 clipped - its own row |
| 4, 5, 6 | `78` | the pipeline reciprocal w, `0x78` at this quad's w = 1 ([[Fragment Shader ISA]], 6.3.2) |
| 7 | `00` | zero |
| 8..15, 63 | `D7` | the r1 value |
| 16 | `EF` | the r0 value (16 reads what 0 reads) |
| 20 | `78` | the r4 value (20 reads what 4 reads) |

The same sweep with r0 = 4.0, r1 empty and r3 = 1.0 (`mfu-src-sweep-...063356Z`,
22 variants, 70 runs) returns `E7`, the r0 value, for 8..16 and 63, and `78` for
5, 6 and 20. So indices at or above 8 do not name r8..r15: they read whichever
seeded value is larger, and with only one row seeded they read that row.

`tests/grate/asm/mfu_high_group.in` pins that down with `rcp r10` at three
seedings (`mfu-high-group-20261125T063822Z`, marker `E7` in the high lane):

| variant | r0 | r1 | word | low lane |
|:--|:--|:--|:--|:--|
| `both` | 4.0 | 16.0 | `0xE700D700` | 16.0 |
| `swap` | 16.0 | 4.0 | `0xE700D700` | 16.0 |
| `only-r0` | 4.0 | - | `0xE700E700` | 4.0 |
| `only-r1` | - | 16.0 | `0xE700D700` | 16.0 |

The value follows the number, not the row: moving 16.0 from r1 to r0 leaves the
result at `D7`. A write to r10 itself never reaches it. `mfu_r10_seed.in` seeds
r10 with 4.0, 1.0, 0.5, and not at all, then runs `sfu: rcp r10` with
`mul0: r0, sfu, #1` and `mul1: r1, sfu, #1` (`mfu-r10-seed-...063935Z`):

| variant | word |
|:--|:--|
| `seed4-rcp-r10`, `seed1-rcp-r10`, `seed-half-rcp-r10`, `unseeded-rcp-r10` | `0xFF00FF00` |
| `seed4-rcp-r4` | `0x78007800` |
| `seed4-sfu-nop` (`sfu: nop r0`) | `0x00000000` |

Four different seeds give the same saturated word, while the same program with
`rcp r4` returns the documented `0x78` in both lanes and the no-sfu control
returns zero in both lanes, so the lanes really are reading the SFU result and
the write to r10 is simply not visible to it.

The value is also not stable across program shapes. With the store moved one
batch later (`r10_clean.in`, `mul0: r2, sfu, #1`, store of r2 with a 0.5 marker,
`r10-clean-...`), `rcp r10` returns `0xFF` for the 4.0 seed, the 1.0 seed and no
seed alike, while `rcp r0` of a seeded 4.0 returns `0xEF00E700` and `rcp r4`
returns `0xEF007800`. Both shapes are deterministic over three repeats and they
disagree with each other.

**Corrected by note 54.** They do not disagree: this program never seeds r0 or
r1, so the selector reads 0 and the 0.5 marker, and `rcp(0.5) = 2.0` prints
`FF`. With the same seeds the two shapes return identical words (54.2).

- Confirmed: indices 0..3 are the row registers, and the unit computes on the
  `.lh` (fp20) value they hold; a write to r10 through the ALU is invisible to
  `sfu: rcp r10`; the value behind indices at or above 8 is not a row.
- Confirmed: index 4, 5, 6 and 20 return the pipeline value (`0x78` at this
  quad's w = 1), 7 returns zero, 16 reads what 0 reads.
- Unknown: what the value behind indices at or above 8 actually is, and why it
  tracks the larger seeded temp in one shape and saturates in another. It is
  deterministic, so it is unit state and not stale register content.
  **Resolved by note 54:** it is `max(|r0|, |r1|, |r2|)`, the cube major-axis
  reciprocal; r3 and r5 are excluded.
- Not required for GLES2: which TEX field would select a cube face; the harness
  cannot bind a cube map (note 51), so that path stays unmeasured.

### 53.3 The two multipliers in the same word

`tests/grate/asm/mul_probe.in` seeds rows, runs one MFU word, and stores one
batch later (`mul-probe-...064727Z`):

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `id-1.0` | `0xF700F700` | multiplying by `#1` keeps 1.0 |
| `id-1.5` | `0xFB00FB00` | keeps 1.5 |
| `id-1.5-rcp` (with `sfu: rcp r3`) | `0xFB00FB00` | kept with an sfu present |
| `muls-only` | `0xFB00FB00` | kept with no sfu sub-instruction at all |
| `rowxrow` (0.75 x 0.5, 0.5 x 0.5) | `0xE700EB00` | 0.375 and 0.25, exact |

So a row operand multiplies correctly, operand order does not matter, and the
multiplier writes an exact fp20 code. Now the compiler's own word. Seeds
r0 = 1.5, r1 = 1.0, r2 = 0.5; expected r0 = 0.75 (`F3`) and r1 = 0.5 (`EF`)
(`mfu-word-x-20261125T064727Z`, and the same word in
`mfu-uniform-mul-...063940Z`):

| variant | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| `word-nosfu` (`mul0: r0, r2, r0`) | `0xEF00F300` | 0.75, 0.5 - the disassembly is right |
| `order-ab` (`mul0: r0, r0, r2`) | `0xEF00F300` | operand order irrelevant |
| `word-r4` (`sfu: rcp r4` + the same two muls) | `0xEF00F300` | a valid sfu source does not disturb them |
| `word-r10` (`sfu: rcp r10` + the same two muls) | `0xF1000000` | not the products |
| `word-r10-order-ab` | `0xF1000000` | same word again |

`mfu_uniform_mul3.in` confirms the last two rows are not an artifact of one
seeding (`mfu-uniform-mul3-...064610Z`):

| r2 | word | reading |
|:--|:--|:--|
| 0.5 | `0xF1000000` | not 0.75, 0.5 |
| 0.25 | `0xF1000000` | identical to the 0.5 case |
| -0.5 | `0xF100E900` | not -0.75, -0.5 |
| 2.0 | `0xEF00F300` | the 0.5 case's products |
| `seed-echo` (multiply by `#1`, `sfu: nop r3`) | `0xF700FB00` | the seeds, exact |

With `sfu: rcp r10` in the word the multipliers stop depending on r2 in any
proportional way - 0.5 and 0.25 return the same word - and r2 = 2.0 reproduces
what r2 = 0.5 returns in a word with no sfu. With `sfu: rcp r4` or no sfu at
all the same two multiplies are exact.

- Confirmed: the multipliers read and write rows correctly; the compiler's
  operand order is fine.
- Confirmed: the identical two multiplies return `0xF1000000` when the word's
  sfu sub-instruction names r10, and the predicted `0xEF00F300` when it names r4
  or when there is no sfu sub-instruction.
- Unknown: the mechanism. The `reg` field is six bits and 10 encodes cleanly
